Title: Automorphisms of Bestvina-Brady Groups: IA Rigidity, Arithmetic Commensurability, and Finiteness

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.23380v1

Published Time: Tue, 28 Jul 2026 00:47:43 GMT

Number of Pages: 101

Markdown Content:
AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS: IA RIGIDITY, ARITHMETIC COMMENSURABILITY, AND FINITENESS 

JIALIN LEI 

Abstract. Let HΓ be the Bestvina–Brady group associated to a finite connected graph 

Γ. For a biconnected defining graph we prove two structure theorems. First, restriction induces an isomorphism IAut( AΓ) ∼= IAut( HΓ), compatible with the Andreadakis–Johnson filtrations. Second, the quadratic and cubic lower-central relation spaces together with the separator arrangement detected by the Bieri–Neumann–Strebel invariant determine a rational associative algebra CΓ. Every integral rank-one square-zero element of this algebra is realized by an automorphism of HΓ, and the resulting all-root subgroup has finite index both in the cohomological image of Aut( HΓ) and in the unit group of an integral order in CΓ.For an arbitrary connected graph, the graph-block decomposition gives the Grushko decomposition of HΓ. Relative free-product automorphism theory then implies that Aut( HΓ)

and Out( HΓ) are finitely generated and satisfy the Tits alternative relative to virtually polycyclic groups. We prove that Aut( HΓ) is finitely presented if and only if Out( HΓ) is finitely presented. The higher finiteness analog fails without additional hypotheses: for 

Γm = Cm ∨ K3, m ≥ 5, one has Out( HΓm ) of type F∞ but Aut( HΓm ) of type F3 and not F4.We also show that HCn is not finitely presented for n ≥ 5, whereas Out( HCn ) is virtually infinite cyclic.

1. Introduction 

1.1. Bestvina–Brady groups and their automorphisms. Let Γ be a finite graph. Its right-angled Artin group and all-ones character are 

AΓ = ⟨V (Γ) | [u, v ] = 1 whenever uv ∈ E(Γ) ⟩ , χΓ : AΓ −→ Z, χΓ(v) = 1 ,

and the associated Bestvina–Brady group is HΓ = ker χΓ. The finiteness properties of HΓ

are governed by the topology of the flag complex of Γ. In particular, HΓ is finitely generated exactly when Γ is connected, and it is finitely presented exactly when the flag complex is simply connected. More generally, for every n ≥ 2 there are finitely generated Bestvina–Brady groups that are not of type Fn [3]. The results below give a uniform description of these automorphism groups. When Γ is biconnected, Aut (HΓ) is an extension of IAut (AΓ) by its cohomological image, and that image is commensurable with the unit group of an explicit order in a rational associative algebra. For an arbitrary connected graph, the Grushko decomposition of HΓ leads to the corresponding relative free-product automorphism group. The IA kernel and the cohomological quotient are determined by different arguments. Restric-tion identifies the IA kernel with IAut (AΓ). The cohomological quotient is not generated solely by restrictions of ambient RAAG automorphisms: it also contains sector shears , constructed directly from the all-power circuit presentation of HΓ. The low-degree invariants determine the possible integral rank-one square-zero operators, and Theorem 5.11 realizes each such operator by a composition of height-preserving ambient automorphisms and sector shears. The resulting roots generate EΓ, which is later proved to have finite index in the actual cohomological image. 1.2. The biconnected case: IA rigidity and arithmetic commensurability. Assume that Γ is biconnected and has at least three vertices. 

Theorem 1.1. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. Then restriction induces an isomorphism 

IAut( AΓ) ∼=

−−→ IAut( HΓ). (1.1) To state the second result, put 

WΓ = H1(HΓ; Z), ΛΓ = im(Aut( HΓ) −→ GL( WΓ)) ,

where we use the homomorphic contragredient convention fixed in Section 2. From the quadratic and cubic lower-central relations and the BNS separator arrangement we construct a rational associative algebra CΓ ≤ End (WΓ ⊗ Q) and its integral order OΓ = CΓ ∩ End (WΓ). Let EΓ be the subgroup generated by all I + N with N ∈ O Γ integral, of rank one, and satisfying N 2 = 0 .

Theorem 1.2. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. Then

Here “explicit” has a precise, limited meaning: one can compute CΓ, its Jacobson radical and Wedderburn block sizes, and one can construct an automorphism realizing any prescribed integral root. We do not claim an algorithm, or a uniform bound, producing a finite root-generating family for EΓ.It follows that, for biconnected Γ, both automorphism groups of HΓ are finitely generated. More precisely, Day’s generators [ 16 , Theorem B], together with Wade’s normalization and abelianization calculation [ 49 , Definition 2.4, Theorems 2.5 and 4.2, Corollary 4.3], restrict to a minimum-cardinality generating set for IAut (HΓ), with the same freely generated abelianiza-tion. The corresponding residual torsion-free nilpotence and orderability results also hold for 

IAut (HΓ) and IOut (HΓ). The IA identification moreover holds filtered: restriction identifies the Andreadakis–Johnson filtrations of IAut (AΓ) and IAut (HΓ) term by term (Theorem 8.11). 1.3. From graph blocks to the global group. Let B2(Γ) be the set of graph blocks with at least three vertices, and let b(Γ) be the number of bridges. The graph-block decomposition induces the following free-product decomposition. 

Theorem 1.4. For every finite connected graph Γ,

HΓ ∼=

and this is the Grushko decomposition: every HB is noncyclic and freely indecomposable. A finite-index pure subgroup of Out (HΓ) fits into a relative free-product restriction sequence whose quotient is the product of finite-index subgroups of the Out (HB ). In particular, both Aut (HΓ)

and Out( HΓ) are finitely generated. 

The proof of (1.7) starts from the Dicks–Leary presentation: every simple circuit lies in one graph block, so the edge generators and all-power relations separate as a free product. It remains to prove that a biconnected block factor admits no further free splitting. Gilbert’s relative Whitehead generating theorem [ 22 , Theorem 2.20 and Section 1] supplies the kernel generators used below, while the broader relative outer-space framework is due to Guirardel– Levitt [ 25 ]. Section 10 derives from these results the finite wreath quotient and pure restriction sequence used here. Combining Corollary 1.3 with that sequence yields a finite subnormal series and a finite generating set for a finite-index subgroup. We also prove two global consequences. For |V (Γ) | ≥ 3, the group HΓ is one-ended if and only if Γ is biconnected, and it has infinitely many ends if and only if Γ has a cut vertex (Corollary 9.4). The outer IA groups fit into the short exact sequence

1.4. Subgroups, torsion, and higher-rank rigidity. The structural decomposition has consequences beyond finite generation. 

Theorem 1.5 (Tits alternative relative to virtually polycyclic groups) . Let Γ be a finite connected graph. Every subgroup of Aut (HΓ) or Out (HΓ) is virtually polycyclic or contains a subgroup isomorphic to F2.6 J. LEI 

We refer to this property as the strong Tits alternative ; the qualifier always means the version relative to virtually polycyclic groups, not merely the alternative “virtually solvable or contains 

F2.” For a biconnected block we give a criterion for when Out (HΓ) is virtually polycyclic. Its IA condition is the absence of a separating intersection of links, while its arithmetic condition is that every matrix block in (1.6) have size one. For biconnected Γ, the cohomological representation is faithful on finite subgroups. Alevel-three congruence kernel is therefore torsion-free, and both Aut (HΓ) and Out (HΓ) are virtually torsion-free with finite virtual cohomological dimension. The cohomological image has the explicit formula 

vcd(Λ Γ) = dim Q Jac( CΓ) + ∑

> )

. (1.8) The same two-step description yields higher-rank rigidity. For a finite biconnected Γ, we define a cubic rank bound Rcub (Γ) from the largest mr in (1.6) , with a lower bound of two. If G is a real semisimple Lie group with finite center and no compact factors, ∆ < G is an irreducible lattice, and rank R G ≥ Rcub (Γ) , then every homomorphism from ∆ to either 

Aut (HΓ) or Out (HΓ) has finite image. In particular, for such a Γ and all sufficiently large 

d, every homomorphism from SL d(Z) to either Aut (HΓ) or Out (HΓ) has finite image. The rank bound is sharp in general: for Γ = Kn, one has HKn ∼= Zn−1 and Rcub (Kn) = n − 1,while the standard inclusion SL n−1(Z) ↪→ Out (HKn ) ∼= GL n−1(Z) has infinite image. Thus the hypothesis rank R G ≥ Rcub (Γ) cannot uniformly be weakened by one. 1.5. Finiteness comparisons and examples. Finite presentability is more delicate: finite generation of the kernel and quotient does not by itself imply finite presentability of the extension group. 

Theorem 1.6. For every finite connected graph Γ,

Aut( HΓ) is finitely presented ⇐⇒ Out( HΓ) is finitely presented .

If Γ is biconnected and has no separating intersection of links, both groups are finitely presented. 

The equivalence in the first assertion is specific to degree two. In higher degrees, Theorem 14.5 gives an Fn-equivalence when HΓ is itself of type Fn, and Theorem 14.6 removes that hypothesis for biconnected graphs. Neither qualification can be dropped: for m ≥ 5, Theorem 15.10 gives 

Out( HCm∨K3 ) ∈ F∞, Aut( HCm∨K3 ) ∈ F3 \ F4.

Thus there is no unconditional Aut–Out equivalence in type Fn for connected defining graphs once n ≥ 4.The cycle and K3 ∗P4 examples show that finite presentability of HΓ and of its automorphism groups can differ in either direction. If n ≥ 5, then the flag complex of Cn is a circle, so HCn

is not finitely presented. Nevertheless, 

IOut( HCn ) ∼= Z, |ΛCn | < ∞, (1.9) and therefore Out (HCn ) is virtually infinite cyclic. In the opposite direction, for the explicit graph Γ∗ = K3 ∗ P4, where K3 is the edgeless three-vertex graph and P4 is the four-vertex path, the flag complex is contractible and hence HΓ∗ is of type F∞, but both Aut (HΓ∗ ) and 

Out( HΓ∗ ) are finitely generated and not finitely presented. For every nonempty finite graph ∆, the join construction gives 

H∆∗K2

∼= A∆ × Z, (1.10) and the defining graph is biconnected. Hence Aut (A∆) and Out (A∆) embed in the corre-sponding automorphism groups of H∆∗K2 ; when ∆ has no universal vertex, these embeddings AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 7

are retractions (Theorem 15.13). Thus the full automorphism groups can be nonlinear even though their cohomological image is arithmetic up to finite index. Their finiteness behavior is summarized in Table 1. 1.6. Ideas of the proof. The proof is divided into three parts. The logical dependence of the two biconnected structure theorems and the global consequences is summarized by 

(I2, I 3, Σ1) −→ CΓ −→ integral-root realization −→ ΛΓ and O× 

> Γ

share EΓ,

uniform period isolation −→ polygon frames −→ IAut( AΓ) ∼= IAut( HΓ),

the two block theorems −→ Grushko and relative free-product consequences .

The two biconnected block theorems are the principal new results of the paper. The global structure, subgroup, and finiteness theorems are obtained by combining them with relative free-product automorphism theory. The cohomological-image part. Write LΓ,Q = H1(HΓ; Q). The lower-central relation spaces 

Id = ker( Ld(LΓ,Q) −→ gr d(HΓ) ⊗ Q)

are intrinsic. The quadratic space I2 is the embedded graph cycle space. The quotient 

I3/[L, I 2] is spanned by the classes of the cubic coefficients ρ3(C) arising from simple circuits C.Separately, the BNS invariant recovers the subspaces associated to minimal vertex separators. Linearizing these three invariants gives row equations. Degree two makes the row locally constant on the components of Γ − { a, v }. A repeated-letter cubic term (Bau − Baw )[ ev, [ea, e v]] 

forces those local constants to agree across the different components, while a three-letter term propagates equality along edges remote from a. The result is the cubic component algebra 

CΓ. Its derived algebra is spanned by basic commutators that are primitive integral rank-one square-zero matrices with pairwise center–axis incidences in {0, ±1}.The low-degree calculation determines CΓ, but does not show that its integral roots occur in the cohomological image. Roots satisfying the domination condition are realized by height-preserving ambient automorphisms, and the remaining roots are realized by sector shears 

duv 7 −→ z−hu duv zhv .

Their validity is checked directly on every all-power circuit relation; the powers of z telescope around the circuit. Finally, the unit incidences 0, ±1 rule out thin subgroups in the SL 2 factors. An arithmetic argument then proves finite index successively in the semisimple quotient, the unipotent radical, and the split torus quotient. The IA part. Let α ∈ IAut (HΓ). The Servatius block decomposition restricts the possible form of each edge image α(uv −1). Applying α to dN 

> 1

· · · dNm = 1 around a simple circuit and isolating primitive period classes shows that no period class can occur only once. It forces a unique colored polygon frame α(uv −1) = xCu (xCv )−1 on every circuit. The period-isolation theorem is derived from the power-word shortening theorem of Lohrey–Stober–Weiß [ 29 ]; a blow-up turns single-letter periods into composite periods without changing their classes. Whitney’s open-ear decomposition [ 51 ] then identifies the circuit frames along the ears and produces a global family (xv)v∈V (Γ) . Sending v to xv defines an IA automorphism of AΓ

extending α. An edge centralizer intersection proves uniqueness, yielding (1.1). The global part. The Dicks–Leary presentation separates over graph blocks because every simple circuit lies in a unique block. The intrinsic BNS invariant computed in Part 1 shows that the factors of the biconnected blocks are freely indecomposable. The resulting Grushko decomposition places the problem within the relative Whitehead theory of Gilbert [ 22 , The-orem 2.20 and Section 1] and the relative outer-space framework of Guirardel–Levitt [ 25 ]. Horbez’s free-product Tits alternative [ 24 , Theorem 6.1] supplies the subgroup dichotomy. 8 J. LEI 

Combining the block exact sequences with the relative restriction sequence gives the global finite-generation and subgroup results. 1.7. Previous work and outline. Bestvina and Brady introduced the groups HΓ and related their finiteness properties to the topology of flag complexes [ 3 ]. Dicks and Leary supplied the all-power edge presentation used throughout this paper [ 18 ]. On the ambient side, Servatius established the centralizer structure used here [ 43 ], while Laurence proved the standard generating theorem for Aut (AΓ) [28 ]. The separating-intersection-of-links condition used below was introduced for graph-product automorphisms by Charney–Ruane–Stambaugh– Vijayan [ 14 , Definition 3.2]. Day supplies the IA generators [ 16 , Theorem B], and Wade supplies the normalization, abelianization, Johnson-filtration, and higher-rank-lattice results used below [ 49 , Definition 2.4, Theorems 2.5, 4.2, 4.6 and 7.3, Corollary 4.3]. The Bieri– Neumann–Strebel invariant was introduced in [ 8]; the BNS argument here uses the RAAG criterion of Meier–VanWyk [ 30 ] and the unrestricted Bestvina–Brady reconstruction theorem of Kochloukova–Mendonça [ 26 , Corollary 1.3]. Chang–Ruffoni obtain related reconstruction and one-endedness results in the finite-presentability range [ 12 , Proposition 4.10, Corollaries 4.21– 4.22]. The present paper proves that these low-degree and BNS invariants determine an explicit associative algebra and that every integral root of this algebra is realized by an automorphism of HΓ.Part 1 identifies the cohomological image. Section 3 computes the intrinsic relation spaces and separator arrangement; Section 4 proves the row-component theorem; Section 5 realizes every integral root; and Section 6 proves finite-index arithmeticity. Part 2 proves IA rigidity in Section 7, combines the IA and cohomological descriptions in Section 8, and concludes with the cycle family in Subsection 8.7. Part 3 begins with the Grushko decomposition and the global relative automorphism structure in Sections 9 and 10. Sections 11, 12, and 13 establish the Tits alternative relative to virtually polycyclic groups, the torsion and virtual-cohomological-dimension results, and higher-rank rigidity. Section 14 gives the Aut–Out finiteness comparisons and the SIL-free sufficient condition, and Section 15 concludes with finite-presentation counterexamples, higher-degree sharpness, universality, and nonlinearity. Subsection 4.9 gives a seven-vertex example showing that the cubic condition is independent of the quadratic and BNS separator conditions. Appendix A derives the period-isolation proposition used in the IA argument, including uniform preprocessing and successive shortening. 2. Preliminaries 

In this paper, all graphs are finite and simple. For group elements we use [x, y ] = xyx −1y−1.Lie brackets are written with the same notation; the meaning is determined by context. The letters AΓ and HΓ always denote the ambient right-angled Artin group and its all-ones Bestvina–Brady group, while matrices always act on cohomology through the homomorphic contragredient convention described below. 2.1. Graphs, separators, and blocks. For a vertex v of a graph Γ, write 

lk( v) = {u : uv ∈ E(Γ) }, st( v) = lk( v) ∪ { v}.

If S ⊆ V (Γ) , then Γ − S denotes the induced subgraph on V (Γ) \ S. A vertex separator is a set S for which Γ − S is disconnected, and it is minimal when no proper subset of S is a separator. Minimality here is inclusion-minimality, not minimal cardinality. A connected graph with at least three vertices is biconnected if Γ − { v} is connected for every vertex v. Equivalently, it has no cut vertex. In particular, every vertex of a biconnected AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 9

graph has degree at least two, and every edge lies on a simple circuit. An edge is a bridge if deleting it disconnects the graph. For a connected graph, a graph block is a maximal subgraph that is either biconnected with at least three vertices or consists of a single bridge and its endpoints. Distinct blocks meet in at most one vertex, and every simple circuit lies in a unique biconnected block. We write 

B2(Γ) for the set of blocks with at least three vertices and b(Γ) for the number of bridges. This terminology is unrelated to the Servatius blocks of a cyclically reduced RAAG element used in Section 7. The join Γ1 ∗ Γ2 is obtained from the disjoint union by adding every edge between V (Γ 1)

and V (Γ 2). It satisfies AΓ1∗Γ2

∼= AΓ1 × AΓ2 .2.2. Right-angled Artin and Bestvina–Brady groups. The right-angled Artin group associated to Γ is 

AΓ = ⟨V (Γ) | [u, v ] = 1 whenever uv ∈ E(Γ) ⟩ . (2.1) The all-ones character is 

χΓ : AΓ −→ Z, χΓ(v) = 1 (v ∈ V (Γ)) .

We call χΓ(g) the height of g ∈ AΓ and refer to χΓ as the height character . The associated Bestvina–Brady group is HΓ = ker χΓ. When Γ is connected, HΓ is generated by the oriented edge differences duv = uv −1 for uv ∈ E(Γ) . Indeed, fix a vertex t. Along an edge path from t to 

v, the corresponding edge differences multiply to vt −1. These elements together with t generate 

AΓ, and the quotient obtained by killing all edge differences identifies every vertex with t,hence is infinite cyclic. Thus their normal closure is ker χΓ; the Dicks–Leary presentation below shows that they already generate it as a group. We use the following theorem of Dicks and Leary [18]. 

Theorem 2.1 (Dicks–Leary) . Let Γ be connected. The group HΓ has one generator duv for each oriented edge (u, v ), with 

dvu = d−1 

> uv

,

and, for every directed closed edge path 

C = ( v0, v 1, . . . , v m = v0)

and every n ∈ Z, the all-power relation 

d nv0v1 d nv1v2 · · · d nvm−1vm = 1 . (2.2) The all-power relation (2.2) already holds in AΓ: adjacent endpoints commute, so d nvi−1vi =

v ni−1v−ni , and the product telescopes around the closed edge path. The nontrivial content of the Dicks–Leary theorem [ 18 ] is that the oriented-edge inverse relations and these all-power relations are complete. The use of every integer power is essential. Sections 3 and 7 extract, respectively, the quadratic and cubic lower-central coefficients and the nonlinear period-matching information from the same family of relations. 2.3. 2.4. Automorphisms and the matrix convention. For a group G, set 

IAut( G) = ker(Aut( G) → GL( H1(G; Z))) , IOut( G) = im (IAut( G) → Out( G)).

Since inner automorphisms act trivially on abelianization, Inn( G) ≤ IAut( G) and IOut( G) = IAut( G)/ Inn( G).We call IAut (G) the IA subgroup of Aut (G)—the automorphisms acting trivially on the abelianization—and IOut (G) the corresponding outer IA group ; in the RAAG literature these are also called the Torelli subgroup and the outer Torelli group [ 16 , 49 ], by analogy with the mapping class group. We use the standard finiteness notation: type Fn means that a group has a classifying space with finite n-skeleton, type F∞ means type Fn for every n, and type F means that the group admits a finite classifying space [21, Ch. 7]. Matrices in this paper act on cohomology. For α ∈ Aut( HΓ), define 

ρW (α) = ( α−1)∗ ∈ GL( WΓ). (2.5) This is a homomorphism rather than an antihomomorphism. We write 

ΛΓ = im( ρW : Aut( HΓ) → GL( WΓ)) .

Relative to dual bases, the action α∗ on LΓ is ρW (α)−T. Every later transpose or inverse is governed by this convention. AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 11 

2.5. Orders, roots, and commensurability. If B is a finite-dimensional unital rational algebra acting on a lattice M , an order in B is a unital subring that is a full Z-lattice in B.Its unit group is an arithmetic subgroup of B× with respect to this integral model [ 5 ]. Two groups are commensurable if they contain isomorphic finite-index subgroups; in this paper the commensurability statement will be stronger, because the relevant groups share the same finite-index subgroup inside GL( WΓ).Section 4 constructs the cubic component algebra CΓ ⊆ End (WΓ,Q). Its integral order is 

OΓ = CΓ ∩ End (WΓ), and its unit group is O× 

> Γ

. If a nonzero rank-one endomorphism is written as R = ασ T, we call α a center vector , σ an axis covector , and σ(β) the center–axis incidence 

with another center vector β. A nonzero element N ∈ O Γ with rank Q N = 1 and N 2 = 0 is called an integral root . The all-root subgroup is generated by the corresponding unipotent elements: 

EΓ =

> 〈

I + N : N ∈ O Γ, rank Q N = 1 , N 2 = 0 

> 〉

. (2.6) Since (I + N )−1 = I − N , every displayed generator is a unit. The three groups EΓ, ΛΓ, and O× 

> Γ

must not be conflated. Section 5 proves EΓ ≤ ΛΓ, while the definition gives EΓ ≤ O × 

> Γ

. No global containment between ΛΓ and O× 

> Γ

is asserted. The only inclusions used are 

EΓ ≤ ΛΓ, EΓ ≤ O × 

> Γ

;

Section 6 proves that both indices are finite. Thus commensurability is obtained through the common subgroup EΓ, not by placing either of the other two groups inside the other. 

Part 1. The cohomological representation of a biconnected block 

3. Intrinsic quadratic, cubic, and separator invariants 

Let Γ be a finite connected graph. In this section, write H = HΓ and U = QV (Γ) , and put 

L = LΓ,Q = ker( ϵ : U → Q), ϵ(ev) = 1 (v ∈ V (Γ)) .

No finite-presentability assumption is made. We first recover from the abstract group H its graphic cycle space and its exact cubic relation space, and then combine these lower-central invariants with the separator arrangement detected by Σ1(H).3.1. Intrinsic lower-central relation spaces. For a group G, let γ1G = G and γd+1 G =[G, γ dG] denote the lower central series, and write gr γ (G) = ⊕ 

> d≥1

γdG/γ d+1 G for the associated graded Lie algebra. Let L(L) = ⊕ 

> d≥1

Ld(L) be the free graded Lie algebra over Q. The identification of L with Hab ⊗ Q induces a graded epimorphism 

π : L(L) −→ gr γ (H) ⊗ Q.

We write 

Id = ker (π : Ld(L) → (γdH/γ d+1 H) ⊗ Q). (3.1) Thus every Id is intrinsic to H. More explicitly, if α ∈ Aut (H) induces g = α∗ ⊗ Q on L,then, writing πd for the degree-d component of π, naturality of the lower central series gives 

πd ◦ g(d) = gr d(α) ◦ πd. Here g(d) is the automorphism induced on Ld(L) and gr d(α) is the induced automorphism of (γdH/γ d+1 H) ⊗ Q. Since these two maps are invertible, the identity implies g(d)(Id) = Id.The comparison with the ambient RAAG is valid in every degree at least two. 

Proposition 3.1. For every finite connected graph Γ,

γq(AΓ) = γq(HΓ) (q ≥ 2) .12 J. LEI 

Proof. Choose a vertex t. By Proposition 2.2, AΓ = H ⋊ ⟨t⟩.We first determine the action of the deck generator on Hab . If uv ∈ E(Γ) and duv = uv −1 ∈ H,then, on H, cu = cduv ◦ cv, where cx denotes conjugation by x. Since cduv is inner in H,conjugation by u and conjugation by v induce the same automorphism of Hab . Connectivity of Γ therefore implies that conjugation by every vertex induces one common automorphism of 

Hab .For an oriented edge uv , the vertices u and v commute, and hence cu(duv ) = duv . The common vertex-conjugation action therefore fixes the class of every oriented edge difference. These edge differences generate H, so their classes generate Hab . The common action is therefore the identity. In particular, conjugation by t acts trivially on H/γ 2H, and hence 

[t, H ] ⊆ γ2H.The subgroup γ2H is characteristic in H and hence normal in AΓ. The preceding inclusion implies that the quotient AΓ/γ 2H is abelian: the image of H is abelian and the image of t

centralizes it. Therefore γ2AΓ ≤ γ2H, while the reverse inclusion γ2H ≤ γ2AΓ is automatic. Thus γ2AΓ = γ2H.Let τ = ct|HΓ . The automorphism induced by τ on gr γ (H) is the identity. Indeed, gr γ (H)

is generated as a graded Lie algebra by its degree-one part: the quotient γqH/γ q+1 H is generated by the images of iterated q-fold commutators of elements of H. Since τ is the identity on H/γ 2H, it fixes all of these generators. Equivalently, for every q ≥ 1 and x ∈ γqH,

txt −1x−1 ∈ γq+1 H. Hence 

[t, γ qH] ⊆ γq+1 H (q ≥ 1) . (3.2) We now argue by induction on q ≥ 2. The equality in degree two is the base case. Suppose that γqAΓ = γqH. Since γq+1 H is characteristic in the normal subgroup H, it is normal in AΓ,so we may pass to AΓ/γ q+1 H. In this quotient the image of γqH commutes with the image of H, because [H, γ qH] = γq+1 H, and it commutes with the image of t by (3.2) . Since AΓ

is generated by H and t, the image of γqH is central. More explicitly, [H, γ qH] = γq+1 H by definition, while [t, γ qH] ≤ γq+1 H by (3.2). Hence 

γq+1 AΓ = [ AΓ, γ qAΓ]= [ AΓ, γ qH]= ⟨[H, γ qH], [t, γ qH]⟩≤ γq+1 H. 

The reverse inclusion follows from H ≤ AΓ. Thus γq+1 AΓ = γq+1 H, completing the induction.

We now prove that the relations obtained from the all-power presentation are all the cubic relations. This is a direct intersection calculation, not a dimension count and not an appeal to finite presentability. The proof first writes I3

as L3(L) ∩ [U, R ]. After choosing U = Qτ ⊕ L and a section of the edge-boundary map, we separate this intersection by the number of occurrences of τ . The terms of τ -degree two and one determine the cancellation constraints; modulo [L, I 2], the remaining datum is a section-defect map evaluated on circuit generators of I2.

In particular, every automorphism of H preserves both I2 and I3. Because an IA automor-phism acts trivially on the associated graded Lie algebra, composition with an IA automorphism cannot change whether a linear map preserves I2 or I3.3.6. The BNS separator arrangement. We use the Bieri–Neumann–Strebel invariant in its original character-sphere convention [ 8 ]. For a finite-dimensional real vector space V , write 

S(V ) = ( V \ { 0})/R>0. This is the character-sphere convention: opposite rays are not identified. For a nonzero subspace Y ≤ V , let S(Y ) denote its image in S(V ). The rational character space is WΓ,Q = L∗ ∼= U/ Q1. For a vertex set S ⊆ V (Γ) , define 

DS = span Q{ev − ew : v, w ∈ S} ≤ L, 

WS = {[x] ∈ U/ Q1 : xv = xw for all v, w ∈ S}. (3.23) Thus WS = D⊥ 

> S

; write WS, R = WS ⊗Q R. A vertex separator is a set S for which the full subgraph on V (Γ) \ S is disconnected. 

Lemma 3.5. Let F be an infinite field. If a finite-dimensional F-vector space Z is contained in a finite union of linear subspaces Y1, . . . , Y r of an ambient vector space, then Z ⊆ Yi for some i.Proof. Otherwise every Z ∩ Yi is a proper subspace of Z. Choose a nonzero linear functional fi

on Z that vanishes on Z ∩ Yi. The product f1 · · · fr is a nonzero polynomial on Z. A nonzero polynomial on a finite-dimensional vector space over an infinite field cannot vanish identically, whereas the assumed covering would make this product vanish at every point of Z. This contradiction proves the lemma. □

Theorem 3.6. For every finite connected graph Γ,

Σ1(HΓ)c = ⋃  

> S⊆V(Γ) Γ[ V(Γ) \S] disconnected

S(WS, R). (3.24) 

If Γ has no cut vertex, the maximal linear members of this union are the WS belonging to inclusion-minimal vertex separators. In that case the abstract group HΓ intrinsically determines the finite family 

DΓ = {DS : S is an inclusion-minimal vertex separator }. (3.25) 

Every automorphism of HΓ permutes this family under its action on L. If Γ has a cut vertex, then Σ1(HΓ) = ∅; no recovery of the other minimal separators is asserted in that case. Proof. A real character of H is represented by a vertex labeling x ∈ RV (Γ) , modulo addition of a constant labeling. Its extensions to AΓ are precisely x + r1, with r ∈ R. The Bestvina–Brady reconstruction theorem [26, Corollary 1.3] (see also [12, Proposition 4.10]) gives 

[x] ∈ Σ1(H) ⇐⇒ [x + r1] ∈ Σ1(AΓ) for every r ∈ R. (3.26) By the Meier–VanWyk RAAG criterion [ 30 ], a character of AΓ lies outside Σ1(AΓ) exactly when its zero-labeled vertices contain a full vertex separator. For a fixed separator S, some r

makes xv + r = 0 for every v ∈ S if and only if x is constant on S, equivalently [x] ∈ W S, R.Substituting this observation in (3.26) proves (3.24). If v is a cut vertex, then S = {v} occurs in the union and W{v} = WΓ,Q. Thus (3.24) is the whole character sphere and Σ1(H) = ∅. The union then has only the whole character space as a maximal linear member, so it need not detect any other minimal separator. Suppose now that Γ has no cut vertex. Every minimal separator then has at least two vertices. By Lemma 3.5, every linear subspace contained in the finite union (3.24) is contained in one of the displayed WT . Every separator T contains an inclusion-minimal separator S,18 J. LEI 

and S ⊆ T gives WT ⊆ W S . Thus every linear member of the union is contained in a member indexed by a minimal separator. It remains only to see that two such members do not contain one another. For |S| ≥ 2,the root space DS determines S: the union of the supports of its nonzero vectors is exactly 

S. Hence WS ⊆ W T implies DT ⊆ DS and therefore T ⊆ S. If both S and T are minimal separators, this forces S = T . The maximal linear members are therefore exactly those claimed. The BNS invariant is intrinsic and an automorphism acts linearly on the character sphere. It therefore permutes these maximal members; taking annihilators gives the permutation of (3.25). □

Remark 3.7 . The union formula requires only connectedness. Recovery and permutation of the full family DΓ require the absence of a cut vertex. In the remainder of Part 1 they are used only for finite biconnected graphs with at least three vertices. 3.7. The combined intrinsic stabilizer. For the remainder of this section, assume that Γ

is a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. In particular, it has no cut vertex, so Theorem 3.6 recovers and permutes the entire family DΓ. We now put the three intrinsic invariants together without identifying homology and cohomology. For g ∈ GL (WΓ,Q), let 

ˇg = ( g−1)∗ ∈ GL (L); in dual bases, ˇg = g−T, as fixed in Section 2. Let ˇg(d) denote its induced action on Ld(L), and define 

GΓ =

> {

g ∈ GL( WΓ,Q) : ˇg(2) I2 = I2, ˇg(3) I3 = I3,

ˇgD S = DS for every inclusion-minimal separator S

> }

. (3.27) This is a rational algebraic subgroup: each condition is the stabilizer of a rational subspace in a finite-dimensional rational representation, and there are only finitely many separator spaces. Let 

ΛΓ,sep = ker(Λ Γ −→ Sym( DΓ)) ,

where the permutation is computed through the contragredient homology action. Functori-ality of the lower-central series implies that this homology action preserves I2 and I3, while Theorem 3.6 shows that it permutes the finite family DΓ. Therefore 

[Λ Γ : Λ Γ,sep ] < ∞, ΛΓ,sep ≤ GΓ(Q) ∩ GL( WΓ). (3.28) Equivalently, the full image lies in the finite setwise extension obtained from (3.27) by allowing a permutation of DΓ.Equation (3.28) is only an invariant-theoretic upper bound; finite generation will instead follow from the root-realization and arithmeticity theorems. 

Remark 3.8 (The cubic condition is essential) . For the seven-vertex graph of Section 4.9, the elements gn = I + nY preserve I2 and every separator space but move I3 for n̸ = 0 ; see (4.37) and (4.41). Thus the quadratic and separator data alone have a strictly larger stabilizer. 4. The cubic component algebra 

Throughout this section, Γ = ( V, E ) is a finite biconnected simple graph with at least three vertices. We first derive the exact infinitesimal row equations forced by the intrinsic quadratic, cubic, and separator invariants of Section 3. The calculation is carried out on homology; the matrix convention relating it to rational cohomology is the contragredient convention fixed in Section 2. AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 19

Theorem 4.1. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices, let X ∈ End (L),and let B be any row lift of X. Then X ∈ ghΓ if and only if, for every a ∈ V , row a of B is constant on each component of P(3)

(4.5) 

Example 4.2 (The five-cycle) . Let Γ = C5 with vertices 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 in cyclic order and take 

a = 0 . The neighborhood hyperedges in H(3) 0 are {1, 3} and {2, 4}; the minimal-separator hyperedges not containing 0 include {1, 3}, {1, 4}, {2, 4}; and the unique edge remote from 0 is 

{2, 3}. These hyperedges connect all four vertices 1, 2, 3, 4, while {0} remains isolated. Hence 

P(3) 0 = {{0}, {1, 2, 3, 4}}, so row 0 of a homological lift is constant away from its diagonal entry. By cyclic symmetry the same description holds at every vertex. Thus the cubic propagation relations identify the quadratic local constants on all four vertices distinct from 0.The proof occupies the next three subsections. It first derives the exact quadratic cut–cochain equations, then proves the two cubic propagation rules, and finally checks that the resulting row equations are sufficient. 20 J. LEI 

4.2. The quadratic cut–cochain equations. Use the doubly centered lift (4.3). By Theo-rem 3.2, identify K = I2 with the space of skew-symmetric edge-supported matrices Ω = ( ωpq )

satisfying Ω1 = 0 . These are precisely the rational circuit flows. The infinitesimal exterior action is Ω 7 −→ BΩ + Ω BT. Its row sums vanish because B1 = 0 and ϵB = 0 . It therefore preserves K exactly when every coefficient on a nonedge vanishes for every circuit flow. Fix a nonedge ij . Its coefficient is 

(BΩ + Ω BT)ij = ∑

> k∼i

Bjk ωik − ∑

> k∼j

Bik ωjk . (4.6) Thus it is the pairing with the edge cochain f Bij defined, with the displayed orientations, by 

f Bij (i, k ) = Bjk , f Bij (j, k ) = −Bik , f Bij (p, q ) = 0 (4.7) on all other edges, extending antisymmetrically under orientation reversal. The annihilator of the graphic cycle space is the cut space. Therefore (4.6) vanishes on K if and only if f Bij is a coboundary. Write Cij = π0(Γ \ { i, j }). Every C ∈ C ij meets both lk (i) and lk (j). For if, say, C had no neighbor of i, then deleting j would separate C from i and from all other components, contrary to biconnectedness; the other assertion is symmetric. A potential for (4.7) is constant on each 

C, because that cochain vanishes on every edge not incident to i or j. Comparing its values at 

i, j, and C gives the following exact criterion. 

Proposition 4.3. For the doubly centered lift B, the condition X[2] I2 ⊆ I2 is equivalent, for every nonedge ij , to the existence of scalars aij C , b ij C for C ∈ C ij and a scalar cij such that 

Bik = aij C (k ∈ lk( j) ∩ C),Bjk = bij C (k ∈ lk( i) ∩ C),aij C + bij C = cij (C ∈ C ij ).

(4.8) 

These equalities, and hence the criterion, are independent of the chosen row lift. Proof. If f Bij = δϕ , use the convention δϕ (p, q ) = ϕ(q) − ϕ(p). Put rC = ϕ|C . Equations (4.7) give bij C = rC − ϕ(i) and aij C = ϕ(j) − rC , so their sum is the constant ϕ(j) − ϕ(i). Conversely, given (4.8) , set ϕ(i) = 0 , ϕ(j) = cij , and ϕ|C = bij C . There are no edges between distinct components of Γ \ { i, j }, and the three equations in (4.8) show directly that f Bij = δϕ . This proves the equivalence. Lift independence follows from (4.2). □

By Jacobi, 

[eu + ev, [τ, e u − ev]] = [ eu, [τ, e u]] − [ev, [τ, e v]] − [τ, [eu, e v]] .

The first two terms telescope around C, as does the sum of eu − ev in the last term of the preceding expansion. The remaining three copies of −[τ, [eu, e v]] give (4.12). Suppose now that X[2] K ⊆ K. The graph Lie algebra is multigraded by vertex multiplicity. We isolate two kinds of homogeneous component of (4.11) . Together they turn the local constants in Proposition 4.3 into the first two families of hyperedges in (4.4). 

Lemma 4.4. If a̸ = v and av / ∈ E, then every row lift of an element of ghΓ satisfies 

Bau = Baw (u, w ∈ lk( v)) . (4.16) 22 J. LEI 

Proof. The assertion is immediate when u = w, so suppose that they are distinct. Since Γ is biconnected, Γ \ { v} is connected. Choose a simple path from w to u in this deleted graph. Together with the edges uv and vw , it gives a simple circuit C, oriented so that its two edges at v are u −→ v −→ w. In FC (B), the component of multidegree ea + 2 ev is 

(Bau − Baw )[ ev, [ea, e v]] . (4.17) Indeed, the edge (u, v ) contributes the first term and (v, w ) contributes the second. No other circuit edge can supply two copies of ev. This remains true if a is another vertex of C: an edge incident to a would bring its other endpoint into the multidegree, and that endpoint cannot be 

v because a and v are distinct and nonadjacent. The Lie monomial in (4.17) is nonzero. The projection killing every generator except ea, e v

is a retraction of LΓ onto the free Lie algebra on these two generators, where [ev, [ea, e v]] ̸ = 0 .Now (4.15) makes (4.17) zero, and (4.16) follows. □

Lemma 4.5. If uv ∈ E and a is adjacent to neither u nor v, then every row lift of an element of ghΓ satisfies 

Bau = Bav . (4.18) 

Proof. A biconnected graph has no bridge, so uv lies on a simple circuit C; orient that circuit through u → v. Among a, u, v , the only graph edge is uv . Hence the only circuit edge that can contribute to multidegree ea + eu + ev in FC (B) is uv itself. Its component in that multidegree is 

Bau [eu, [ea, e v]] + Bav [ev, [eu, e a]] = ( Bau − Bav )[ eu, [ea, e v]] ,

where the equality is Jacobi together with [eu, e v] = 0 .The remaining bracket is nonzero. In the partially commutative tensor algebra its expansion is 

eueaev − euevea − eaeveu + eveaeu. (4.19) When eu and ev commute but neither commutes with ea, the four words in (4.19) represent four distinct trace classes. The standard embedding of the graph Lie algebra into this trace algebra [ 19 ] therefore shows that the bracket is nonzero. Equation (4.15) now gives (4.18) . □

We can already read off the necessity in Theorem 4.1. Lemma 4.4 makes row a constant on every hyperedge lk (v) with a̸ = v and av / ∈ E; Lemma 4.5 does the same on every remote edge {u, v }; and (4.9) gives constancy on every minimal separator not containing a. Equality propagates along intersecting hyperedges, so row a is constant on every component of P(3)  

> a

.4.5. Sufficiency and exactness. Conversely, suppose that a row lift B satisfies (4.5) . Since those equations are lift-independent, replace B by the doubly centered lift without changing them. If ij is a nonedge, the first family in (4.5) , applied with (a, v ) = ( i, j ), makes row i

constant on all of lk (j); call its value aij . Symmetrically, row j has a single value bij on lk (i).For every C ∈ C ij , set aij C = aij , bij C = bij , and cij = aij + bij . These values satisfy (4.8) , so Proposition 4.3 gives X[2] I2 ⊆ I2. The third family of row equations and (4.9) give BD S ⊆ DS

for every minimal separator S.It remains to prove FC (B) = 0 for every simple circuit. We do this in the vertex multigrading. At a circuit vertex v, orient the incident circuit edges as u → v → w. For any a ∈ V , the component of multidegree ea + 2 ev is precisely (Bau − Baw )[ ev, [ea, e v]] , by the calculation in Lemma 4.4. If a = v, the bracket is zero. If av ∈ E, it is zero by partial commutation. Finally, if a̸ = v and av / ∈ E, the first row equation makes its scalar coefficient zero. Thus every repeated-letter component vanishes; this also covers the three-equal-letter case. AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 23 

Now take three distinct vertices a, u, v . A contribution supported on these vertices must come from a circuit edge having two of them as its endpoints. For an oriented circuit edge 

u → v, its component in this multidegree is 

(Bau − Bav )[ eu, [ea, e v]] , (4.20) up to the simultaneous sign change caused by reversing the orientation. This follows exactly as in Lemma 4.5: the two terms with three distinct letters are related by Jacobi and [eu, e v] = 0 .There are now three exhaustive graph-theoretic cases. (i) If the graph induced on {a, u, v } has exactly one edge, that edge is uv . The second row equation makes Bau = Bav , so (4.20) vanishes. (ii) If the induced graph has at least two edges, then in addition to uv at least one of au, av 

is an edge. If av ∈ E, the inner bracket in (4.20) is zero. If au ∈ E, the derivation form of Jacobi gives [eu, [ea, e v]] = [[ eu, e a], e v] + [ ea, [eu, e v]] = 0 . Hence each contributing circuit edge gives zero, whether the induced graph is a two-edge path or a triangle. (iii) The induced graph cannot be edgeless: every summand of FC (B) contains the two endpoints of the circuit edge that produced it. Every homogeneous component of degree three is either repeated-letter or three-distinct-letter, so these cases exhaust FC (B). Therefore FC (B) = 0 for every simple circuit, and (4.15) gives 

X[3] I3 ⊆ I3. We have proved that the row equations imply all three conditions in (4.1). Together with the necessity proved above, this completes the proof of Theorem 4.1. In particular, the partition P(3)  

> a

is exact: its components are generated by, and only by, the neighborhood hyperedges, minimal-separator hyperedges, and remote-edge pairs in (4.4) ; no three-connectivity hypothesis or additional row relation is used. 4.6. 4.7. Primitive commutator roots. Put WZ = ZV /(Z1) ⊂ W . Since the identity is central and the matrices TC,i span CΓ with it, equations (4.25) and (4.28) give

{[TC,i , T D,j ] : i̸ = j}. (4.30) We call the nonzero commutators in this spanning family the basic roots .

Proposition 4.7. Every basic root is an integral primitive rank-one endomorphism of WZ

whose square is zero. Its matrix entries lie in {0, 1, −1}. Moreover, every center–axis incidence between two basic roots belongs to {0, 1, −1}.Proof. Retain the notation α, β from the proof of Proposition 4.6, and write 

R = [ TC,i , T D,j ] = w(ej − ei)T, w = wi,j C,D .

Therefore R2 = ( wj − wi)R = 0 .If R̸ = 0 on W , then both its center class ¯w ∈ W and its axis (ej − ei)T are nonzero, so R has rank one. The center has a more precise integral form. According as (α, β ) is (0 , 0) , (1 , 0) , (0 , 1) , or 

(1 , 1) , one has 

w = −1C∩D, w = 1C\D, w = 1D\C , w = 1C∪D, (4.31) AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 25 

respectively. Thus a nonzero center is, up to sign, the class of 1S for a nonempty proper subset 

S ⊊ V : the empty-set indicator is zero, while the full-set indicator is zero in WZ = ZV /(Z1).The class of 1S is primitive in WZ. Indeed, if [1S ] = n[z], then 1S − nz = m1 for some 

m ∈ Z. Choosing s ∈ S and t / ∈ S and subtracting the corresponding coordinates gives 

1 = n(zs − zt), so n = ±1. Formula (4.28) therefore defines an integral endomorphism of WZ.It is primitive even as a lattice endomorphism: if R = nQ with Q ∈ End (WZ), then evaluation on the class of ej gives ¯w = nQ ( ¯ej ), because (ej − ei)Tej = 1 ; primitivity of ¯w again forces 

n = ±1. Formula (4.31) also shows directly that all entries of R lie in {0, 1, −1}.Finally, let R′ = w′(eℓ − ek)T be another basic root. Its axis evaluates on the center of R as 

(eℓ − ek)Tw = wℓ − wk. By (4.31) , the coordinates of w lie either in {0, 1} or in {0, −1}. Hence this difference, and symmetrically the opposite center–axis incidence, belongs to {0, 1, −1}. □

4.8. The split Wedderburn quotient. Put A = CΓ and J = Jac (A). The inclusion 

A ⊆ End( W ) makes W a faithful finite-dimensional A-module. Choose a composition series 

0 = W0 ⊂ W1 ⊂ · · · ⊂ Wr = W, Wss = gr W =

> r⊕
> ν=1

Wν /W ν−1.

We first establish the module-theoretic lemma needed for the Artin–Wedderburn argument. 

Lemma 4.8. The induced action of A/J on Wss is faithful: 

A/J ↪ → End( Wss ). (4.32) 

Proof. Let N be the kernel of the action of A on Wss . Every element of N sends Wν into Wν−1,so a product of r elements of N annihilates W . Faithfulness of the original action gives N r = 0 .Thus N is a nilpotent two-sided ideal and N ⊆ J. Conversely, the Jacobson radical annihilates every simple A-module, hence every quotient Wν /W ν−1 [ 31 , Ch. 2]; therefore J ⊆ N . Hence 

N = J, proving (4.32). □

Lemma 4.9. If T ∈ A preserves the displayed composition series, then 

rank(gr T ) ≤ rank( T ). (4.33) 

In particular, every basic root has rank at most one on Wss .Proof. Filter ker T by ker T ∩ Wν . The induced map gr (ker T ) → Wss lands in ker (gr T ), so 

dim ker (gr T ) ≥ dim ker T . Rank–nullity gives (4.33) . The last assertion uses Proposition 4.7. 

□

Proposition 4.10. Every noncommutative simple factor of A/J is a full matrix algebra 

Mm(Q), and its isotypic part in Wss consists of one copy of the natural Mm(Q)-module. Proof. By the Artin–Wedderburn theorem [31, Ch. 1], write

k dim Q(D) rank D(s) ≥ k dim Q(D)

on that isotypic part. The projection A ↠ S maps [A, A ] onto [S, S ]̸ = 0 . Since the basic roots span [A, A ] by (4.30) , some basic root R has nonzero image in S. Lemma 4.9 bounds the rational rank of its action on Wss by one. Hence k dim Q(D) ≤ 1, so k = 1 and D = Q. This proves both the split matrix form and the multiplicity-one assertion. □26 J. LEI 

Lemma 4.11. Every commutative simple factor of A/J is Q.Proof. A commutative simple factor is a finite field extension F/ Q. By (4.24) , the algebra A

is spanned by I and the elements TC,i , while (4.25) shows that every −TC,i is an idempotent. Under a homomorphism A → F , each such idempotent has image zero or one. The image of A

is therefore contained in the scalar copy of Q, and surjectivity onto the simple factor forces 

F = Q. □

4.9. A seven-vertex cubic obstruction. We conclude this section with a biconnected example showing that the cubic relation space is not determined by the quadratic cycle space and the BNS separator arrangement. Let V (Γ) = {0, 1, . . . , 6} and 

E(Γ) = {02 , 04 , 05 , 06 , 12 , 14 , 15 , 16 , 23 , 24 , 25 , 34 , 35 }. (4.35) A direct deletion check shows that Γ is biconnected and that its inclusion-minimal vertex separators are exactly

5. Realization of integral rank-one roots 

Throughout this section, Γ = ( V, E ) is a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. We write 

LZ = LΓ = ker( ϵ : ZV → Z), WZ = WΓ = ZV /(Z1),

and use the cohomological matrix convention fixed in Section 2. The goal of the section is to realize the integral rank-one square-zero elements of the cubic component algebra CΓ by actual automorphisms of HΓ. This is stronger than merely checking that a matrix preserves the quadratic, cubic, and separator invariants. Let R ∈ CΓ ∩ End (WZ) be nonzero and of rank one. Since WZ is a free abelian group, choose a primitive generator α of the saturation of im R and absorb the integral content of R into the dual factor. Thus 

R = ασ T, α ∈ WZ, σ ∈ Hom( WZ, Z) = LZ. (5.1) We use the same letter α for an integral representative in ZV ; changing that representative by a multiple of 1 does not change the class in WZ. Direct multiplication gives 

R2 = σ(α)R. (5.2) Therefore R2 = 0 if and only if σ(α) = 0 . No denominator is introduced in (5.1).

5.2. Local equations and ambient domination roots. Return to the integral square-zero root R = ασ T ∈ CΓ from (5.1) , and put Sσ = supp (σ) = {i ∈ V : σi̸ = 0 }. For i ∈ Sσ, column 

i of R is the nonzero scalar multiple σiα. The column form of the row-component theorem in Section 4 therefore gives the following two equations: 

α is constant on lk( y) if i ∈ Sσ, i ̸ = y, and iy / ∈ E, (5.16) 

αx = αy if i ∈ Sσ, xy ∈ E, ix, iy / ∈ E. (5.17) These statements do not depend on the chosen representative of α ∈ WZ. The full-neighborhood assertion in (5.16) , rather than constancy separately on components of Γ \ { i, y }, is exactly where the cubic repeated-letter equation is used. We use the Laurence–Servatius domination preorder on the vertices of Γ [28, 43]: 

k ⪯ i ⇐⇒ lk( k) ⊆ st( i). (5.18) Thus i dominates k, and the elementary matrix I + nE ik is induced on Aab Γ by an nth power of a dominated Laurence–Servatius transvection. Two vertices i and k are domination-equivalent when both k ⪯ i and i ⪯ k.

Lemma 5.3. Let α ∈ ZV and σ ∈ LZ satisfy σ(α) = 0 . Suppose that 

αkσi̸ = 0 =⇒ k ⪯ i. (5.19) 

Then a height-preserving automorphism of AΓ restricts to an automorphism of HΓ whose cohomology action is I + ¯ ασ T.AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 31 

Proof. Consider the ambient homology matrix M = I − σα T ∈ MV (Z). Since αTσ = 0 , its inverse is M −1 = I + σα T. Both matrices have a nonzero (i, k )-entry away from the identity only when k ⪯ i, by (5.19). Let P (Z) be the group of units in the integral structural matrix ring whose (i, k )-entry may be nonzero only when k ⪯ i. We show that P (Z) is generated by full integral GL -groups on domination-equivalence classes and the elementary matrices I + nE ik in strict domination positions. Order the domination-equivalence classes by a linear extension of the quotient partial order. With a maximal class placed last, every structural unit has block form 

> (

A 0

B D

> )

= 

> (

I 0

BA −1 I

> ) (

A 00 D

> )

.

The diagonal blocks A and D are integral units: their nonzero integral determinants have product ±1. The inverse A−1 belongs to the smaller structural ring, so transitivity of domination implies that every entry of BA −1 is still in a strict allowed position. The elementary matrices belonging to that last block row commute with one another and factor the first displayed matrix. Induction on the number of classes proves the claim. Inside a domination-equivalence class, inversions and the two directions of dominated transvections generate its full integral GL -group. Hence every element of P (Z) is induced on abelianization by a word in the Laurence–Servatius generators [28, 43]. The matrix M lies in P (Z). Moreover 1TM = 1T because 1Tσ = 0 . Thus an ambient automorphism inducing M preserves the height character and restricts to HΓ. On LZ the restriction has matrix M |LZ , and the cohomological convention gives (M |LZ )−T = I + ¯ασ T.This proves the lemma. □

p, then q is distinct from and nonadjacent to both endpoints, since Mpq is empty, and (5.17) applies; edges incident to q are symmetric. Suppose neither endpoint is an axis. If one of p, q

constant on lk (y), which contains x and q. Thus αp = αx = αq = αy. Connectivity now makes 

α globally constant. Taking that constant as c proves (5.21) also in this case. □

Proposition 5.5. Suppose that p, q ∈ Sσ are distinct and nonadjacent and that αp̸ = αq. Then 

I + R is realized by the restriction of a height-preserving ambient automorphism of AΓ.Proof. Replace the representative α by α − c1, with c supplied by Lemma 5.4. This does not change R on WZ. Every k ∈ supp( α) is then dominated by both p and q.Fix i ∈ Sσ. If i failed to dominate such a vertex k, there would be a vertex y ∈ lk (k) \ st (i).Domination of k by p and q puts y in both st (p) and st (q). It cannot equal either axis, because 

pq is a nonedge, and hence k, p, q ∈ lk (y). The vertices i and y are distinct and nonadjacent, so (5.16) for the eligible column i makes α constant on lk (y). This contradicts αp̸ = αq. Thus, whenever αkσi̸ = 0 , one has k ⪯ i. Lemma 5.3 now gives the claimed lift. □

We now turn to an axis supported on an edge. The part of the center that deviates from its local neighbor value is ambient; after removing it, the remaining center satisfies the hypotheses of the sector-shear lemma. Let p, q ∈ V be distinct adjacent vertices and put

It remains to verify that these two realized factors multiply to the target, rather than merely adding infinitesimally. Put 

Ra = ¯ aσ T, Rh = ¯hσ T.

Equations (5.30) and (5.33) imply 

RaRh = σ(h) ¯ aσ T = 0 , RhRa = σ(a) ¯hσ T = 0 .

Therefore the order of the two cohomological matrix factors is immaterial (no commutativity assertion about the realizing automorphisms is needed), and 

(I + Ra)( I + Rh) = I + Ra + Rh = I + (¯ a + ¯h)σT = I + ¯ α(ep − eq)T = I + R. 

All exponents and matrix entries are integral by construction. Composing the ambient automorphism with the sector shear therefore realizes I + R exactly. □AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 35

5.6. Completion of the realization theorem. Theorem 5.11. Let Γ be finite and biconnected with at least three vertices. If 

R ∈ CΓ ∩ End( WZ), rank Q R = 1 , R2 = 0 ,

then there exists Φ ∈ Aut( HΓ) such that ρW (Φ) = I + R.Proof. Use the integral factorization R = ασ T from (5.1) . If the axis support contains a distinct nonadjacent pair at different α-levels, Proposition 5.5 realizes the whole root by one height-preserving ambient automorphism. Otherwise the level decomposition (5.36) expresses R as the sum of integral coordinate-axis roots and the clique-axis root R0. Every coordinate root is realized by Proposition 5.6 or Proposition 5.8. If R0̸ = 0 , it is realized by Proposition 5.10; if R0 = 0 , the factor is omitted. Equation (5.40) , or equivalently Lemma 5.1, shows that the corresponding cohomological matrices multiply exactly to I + R. Inside the clique induction, every recursive split is likewise exact by (5.47) , and each terminal factor is an exact ambient–sector product. All centers, axes, and shear exponents remain integral throughout. Composing the realizing automorphisms therefore gives the required Φ. □

Throughout this section, Γ is finite and biconnected with at least three vertices. We first place the all-root subgroup in the derived group of the cubic algebraic unit group, prove its Zariski density there, and prove arithmeticity of its image in the semisimple quotient, including the rank-two factors. We then prove Zariski density and finite index in the integral points of the unipotent radical, analyze the split torus quotient, and compare the resulting finite-index root subgroup with the actual cohomological image. > Γ

be the algebraic unit group. In this section G denotes this connected unit group, whereas GΓ continues to denote the full combined stabilizer from Section 3. It is closed in GL (W ); being the nonvanishing locus of the determinant, it is a nonempty principal open subset of the irreducible affine space underlying CΓ, hence irreducible and in particular Zariski connected, and its Lie algebra is CΓ. Section 4 gives the same Lie algebra for G◦

> Γ

. In characteristic zero, Zariski-connected algebraic matrix groups with the same Lie algebra coincide [ 4]: the identity component of their intersection has the common tangent space, hence full dimension in each group, and a closed subgroup of full dimension in a connected algebraic group is the whole group. torus is a closed torus [ 4]; it has the full dimension of the connected target, so it is the whole target. Thus G/D is a quotient of a split torus and is itself split. Now let N ∈ O Γ have rank one and square zero. The formula uN (t) = I + tN , whose inverse is uN (t)−1 = I − tN , defines a rational homomorphism Ga → G. Lie( K) ⊇ span Q XΓ = [ CΓ, CΓ] = Lie( D).

Since K ≤ D and D is connected, equality of Lie algebras forces K = D. □

Corollary 6.3. Let J = Jac( CΓ). Then 

1 + J ≤ D, J ⊆ [CΓ, CΓ]. (6.13) 

Equivalently, the graph-specific abelian algebraic quotient G/D has no unipotent part. Proof. The group 1 + J is a connected rational unipotent subgroup of G. Proposition 6.2 identifies G/D as a torus, and a torus has no nontrivial unipotent subgroup [ 4]. Hence the image of 1 + J in that quotient is trivial, which proves 1 + J ≤ D. Taking Lie algebras and using (6.9) gives J ⊆ [CΓ, CΓ]. □

Lemma 6.5. If mr ≥ 3, the projection of EΓ to the factor SL mr contains a finite-index elementary arithmetic subgroup with finite abelianization. Proof. In a rational natural basis, the elements in (6.19) project to I + cnE ab for a̸ = b and 

n ∈ Z. Tits’s common-power theorem [ 46 ] says that the group generated by these common-level root groups has finite index in the corresponding integral SL mr .Its abelianization is finite as well. For three distinct indices a, k, b , the elementary commutator identity gives 

[I + cuE ak , I + cvE kb ] = I + c2uvE ab . (6.21) Thus the commutator subgroup contains the parameter-c2Z subgroup of every root group. The group is generated by finitely many parameter-cZ root groups, each of whose images in the abelianization is therefore finite. □

6.3. The unit-incidence argument for SL 2. The common-level conclusion alone is not sufficient in rank one: two opposite high-level cusp groups in SL 2 can generate a thin subgroup, meaning a Zariski-dense subgroup of infinite index in the relevant arithmetic lattice. We now use the integral unit incidences in (6.6) to produce unit parameters. 42 J. LEI 

Lemma 6.6. Fix a factor Gss ,r ∼= SL 2 in the semisimple quotient and write hr ∼= sl 2 for its Lie algebra. There are basic roots X = x ⊗ f and Y = y ⊗ h whose semisimple projections are supported only in the rth factor and satisfy 

tr( XrYr)̸ = 0 . (6.22) 

Moreover, 

f (x) = h(y) = 0 , f (y) = ε ∈ { 1, −1}, h(x) = η ∈ { 1, −1}, (6.23) 

and Xr, Y r generate hr as a Lie algebra. Proof. The semisimple projections of the basic roots active in the rth factor span hr. If the trace product of every two members of this spanning family were zero, the nondegenerate trace form on sl 2 would vanish on all of hr, a contradiction. Choose X, Y satisfying (6.22) . The semisimple-support statement preceding (6.19) says that their semisimple projections vanish in every other simple factor. Relative to the Levi filtration, radical parts are strictly upper block triangular and Levi parts are block diagonal. Mixed products and products of two radical parts therefore have zero trace. Hence 

0̸ = tr( XrYr) = tr( Xss Y ss ) = tr( XY ) = f (y)h(x).

Square-zero gives f (x) = h(y) = 0 , while Proposition 4.7 puts both remaining incidences in 

{0, 1, −1}. Their nonzero product proves (6.23) . Finally, two nonzero nilpotent matrices in 

sl 2 with nonzero trace product are opposite nilpotents; together with their bracket they span 

sl 2. □

Lemma 6.8 (Arithmetic images and integral models) . The following standard facts will be used below. 

(i) A rational isomorphism of linear algebraic Q-groups carries arithmetic subgroups to arithmetic subgroups. 

(ii) A finite direct product of arithmetic subgroups is arithmetic in the corresponding product group. 

(iii) If ρ : H → GL( TQ) is a faithful rational representation and T0 ⊂ TQ is a lattice, then 

ρ(H(Q)) ∩ GL( T0)

is an arithmetic subgroup of the rational group ρ(H).

(iv) Any two arithmetic subgroups of one rational algebraic group are commensurable. AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 43 

Proof. These assertions are standard consequences of the representation-independent definition of arithmeticity. In the concrete lattice formulation used here, changing the lattice changes the integral stabilizer only up to commensurability [ 50 , Proposition 5.4.5], while a rational surjection carries integral points to a subgroup commensurable with the integral points of the target [ 50 , Proposition 9.4.5]. Applying the latter result to a rational isomorphism and its inverse proves (i); (ii) follows by taking the product lattice; and Proposition 5.4.5(1) gives (iii). Finally, after choosing one faithful rational representation of H, every arithmetic subgroup is commensurable with the stabilizer of a lattice, and Proposition 5.4.5(2) proves (iv). See also the general framework in [5, §6]. □

Proposition 6.9. Fix an SL 2 factor of the semisimple quotient. There are a primitive rank-two direct summand of WZ and two basic-root generators in EΓ that generate the full SL 2(Z) on that summand. The Zariski closure of this integral subgroup is a rational SL 2 whose projection to the selected semisimple factor is a rational isomorphism and whose projection to every other factor is trivial. Therefore its image in the selected factor is arithmetic and has finite abelianization; in particular it is neither diagonal across factors nor thin. Proof. Choose X, Y from Lemma 6.6 and use the direct summand from Lemma 6.7. The two unit elementary transvections displayed there generate the full SL 2(Z) on that summand: taking inverses changes either parameter −1 to 1, and the standard unit upper and lower transvections generate SL 2(Z) [42 , Ch. I]. Each of the two integral root subgroups is Zariski dense in its one-parameter unipotent root group, so their Zariski closure is a rational subgroup 

S ∼= SL 2. The differential of the projection S → Gss ,r contains Xr and Yr and hence is all of 

hr; the resulting map is a central isogeny. For every s̸ = r, the differential of 

pr s ◦qss |S : S −→ Gss ,s 

is zero: the Lie algebra of S is generated by the two opposite nilpotents X and Y , whose semisimple projections vanish in the sth factor. Since S is connected, the image of this morphism is a connected zero-dimensional algebraic subgroup and is therefore trivial. Therefore the kernel of the projection to the selected factor lies in 

S ∩ ker qss = S ∩ U.

The kernel is finite because the selected projection is a central isogeny, whereas a rational unipotent group in characteristic zero has no nontrivial finite subgroup. Hence the kernel is trivial. The selected projection is therefore a rational isomorphism, and the projections to all other factors are trivial. The standard SL 2(Z) on the primitive summand is arithmetic in S,so Lemma 6.8(i) makes its image arithmetic in the selected factor. Its abelianization is finite because it is a quotient of the displayed SL 2(Z). □

Proof. Choose bases of L and T . The matrix coefficients of ρ are regular functions on H

ρ(γ)T.

Proposition 6.11. The image Π is an arithmetic subgroup of Gss ; in particular it has finite index in the semisimple image of the integral derived points, and it has finite abelianization: 

[qss (SD) : Π] < ∞, |Πab | < ∞. (6.27) 

Proof. For each factor with mr ≥ 3, take the common-level elementary subgroup from Lemma 6.5; for each factor with mr = 2 , take the arithmetic factor subgroup furnished by the integral SL 2(Z) of Proposition 6.9. Because each Ar is contained in the rth simple factor, their product Ass = ∏  

> {r:mr≥2}

Ar is an arithmetic subgroup of Gss contained in Π, by Lemma 6.8(i)–(ii). We compare this subgroup with the entire image of SD. Choose a faithful rational representa-tion ρ : Gss → GL (TQ) and a lattice T ⊆ TQ. Apply Lemma 6.10 to the rational representation 

ρ ◦ qss of D and the integral subgroup SD. It gives an invariant lattice T0 with 

ρ(qss (SD)) ≤ B := ρ(Gss (Q)) ∩ GL( T0). (6.28) By Lemma 6.8(iii), B is an arithmetic subgroup of ρ(Gss ), and the rational isomorphism onto the faithful image carries the arithmetic group Ass to an arithmetic subgroup ρ(Ass ). The same lemma therefore makes these two subgroups commensurable. Since 

ρ(Ass ) ≤ ρ(Π) ≤ ρ(qss (SD)) ≤ B ,

the subgroup ρ(Ass ) has finite index in B. Faithfulness of ρ now shows that Π has finite index in qss (SD).It remains to check the abelianization assertion. The higher-rank factors Ar have finite abelianization by (6.21). For a rank-two factor, the standard presentation [42, Ch. I] 

SL 2(Z) = ⟨s, t | s4 = 1 , s 2 = ( st )3⟩

gives 4s = 0 and 2s = 3 s + 3 t after abelianization, hence s = −3t and 12 t = 0 . Thus every factor, and therefore Ass , has finite abelianization. Finally, Ass has finite index in Π. Its image in Πab is a quotient of the finite group Aab ss . The quotient by that image is 

Π/([Π , Π] ⟨⟨ Ass ⟩⟩ ),

which is finite because the normal closure of the finite-index subgroup Ass still has finite index in Π. Therefore Πab is finite. □

Lemma 6.12. Put 

JZ = J ∩ End( WZ).

Then 

NU = 1 + JZ. (6.30) 

This is a finitely generated torsion-free nilpotent group, it is Zariski dense in U = 1 + J, and 

hl( NU ) = dim Q J = dim U. (6.31) 

Proof. If u ∈ NU , then u = 1 + j for a unique j ∈ J and the integrality of u gives j ∈ JZ.Conversely, if j ∈ JZ, nilpotence of J gives 

(1 + j)−1 = 1 − j + j2 − · · · ,

a finite polynomial with integral coefficients. Thus 1 + j ∈ NU , proving (6.30). Let Jν = 0 and put 

Lk = Jk ∩ End( WZ), Nk = 1 + Lk (1 ≤ k ≤ ν).

Each Lk is a full lattice in the rational space Jk, and [Ni, N j ] ≤ Ni+j . Moreover, 

Nk/N k+1 ∼= Lk/L k+1 , 1 + x 7 −→ x mod Lk+1 ,

because multiplication is addition modulo Jk+1 . Hence the series N1 ≥ N2 ≥ · · · ≥ Nν = 1 has finitely generated abelian factors. It follows that NU = N1 is finitely generated and nilpotent. A unipotent matrix of finite order in characteristic zero is the identity, so NU is torsion-free. Summing the ranks of the displayed factors gives 

hl( NU ) = ∑

> k

dim Q(Jk/J k+1 ) = dim Q J. 

Finally, the affine isomorphism J → 1 + J, j 7 → 1 + j, carries the full lattice JZ to NU . A full lattice is Zariski dense in its ambient rational vector space, so NU is Zariski dense in U. □

Lemma 6.13. With the notation (6.29) , one has 

[U, D] ≤ U0. (6.32) 

Therefore U0 ◁ D and V = U/U0 is a central rational vector group in D/U0. For a rational Levi subgroup M0 of this quotient there is a rational direct product 

D/U0 ∼= V × M0, M0 ∼= Gss , (6.33) 

and the image of EΓ is the graph of a homomorphism 

c : Π −→ V(Q). (6.34) 

Proof. Lemma 6.12 shows directly that NU is Zariski dense in U. If u ∈ NU and e ∈ EΓ,normality EΓ ◁ O× 

> Γ

and normality of U in D give [u, e ] ∈ EΓ ∩ U(Q) = EU . Fixing e and taking the closure in the first variable gives [U, e ] ≤ U0. Fixing an element of U and using the Zariski density of EΓ in D then gives (6.32) . This inclusion implies normality of U0 and centrality of V. A central connected unipotent group in characteristic zero is a rational vector group [4]. Mostow’s rational Levi decomposition [ 35 ] gives M0, and centrality turns the semidirect product into (6.33) . The image of EΓ meets V trivially: an element in the intersection maps trivially to Gss , hence is represented by an element of EU , which vanishes modulo U0. Its projection to M0 is Π, so it is the graph of the homomorphism in (6.34). □

Lemma 6.14. One has 

U0 = U. (6.35) 46 J. LEI 

Proof. The target of (6.34) is abelian and torsion-free, while Πab is finite by Proposition 6.11. Hence c = 0 . If V were nonzero, the image of EΓ in (6.33) would lie in the proper algebraic subgroup 0 × M0. This contradicts the Zariski density of EΓ in D. Therefore V = 0 . □

For a polycyclic group P , write hl (P ) for its Hirsch length, the number of infinite cyclic factors in a polycyclic series. 

Lemma 6.15. Let A ≤ N be finitely generated nilpotent groups. If hl (A) = hl (N ), then 

[N : A] < ∞.Proof. Induct on the nilpotency class of N . The abelian case is the full-rank lattice statement for finitely generated abelian groups. Otherwise put Z = Z(N ). Additivity of Hirsch length in short exact sequences [40, Chapter 1] gives 

hl( A) = hl( A ∩ Z) + hl( AZ/Z ), hl( N ) = hl( Z) + hl( N/Z ).

The two terms in the first equality are bounded above by the corresponding terms in the second. Equality of the totals forces equality in both bounds. The abelian case gives [Z : A ∩ Z] < ∞,while induction in N/Z gives [N/Z : AZ/Z ] < ∞. Since [AZ : A] = [ Z : A ∩ Z] and 

[N : AZ ] = [ N/Z : AZ/Z ], the result follows. □

Proposition 6.16. One has 

U0 = U, [NU : EU ] < ∞. (6.36) 

Proof. The first assertion is Lemma 6.14. Lemma 6.12 makes NU finitely generated torsion-free nilpotent. Its subgroup EU is therefore finitely generated [40, Chapter 1] and torsion-free. For a finitely generated torsion-free nilpotent subgroup P ≤ U(Q), Mal’cev completion identifies the rational completion of P with the connected rational unipotent subgroup generated by P [ 33 ]; see also [ 39 , Chapter II]. Along an isolated central series, rank and Lie dimension agree, so 

hl( P ) = dim( the rational Mal’cev completion of P ). (6.37) The completion of NU is U, and (6.35) says the same for EU . Hence 

hl( EU ) = dim U = hl( NU ). (6.38) Lemma 6.15 now gives the finite-index assertion. □

Remark 6.22 (Canonicity and functoriality) . Every constituent of (6.53) is intrinsic to HΓ:the lattice WZ, the algebra CΓ with its radical, and the derived unit group are built from the quadratic, cubic, and separator invariants of Section 3. An isomorphism H∆ → HΓ therefore transports the sequence for ∆ to the one for Γ.

Part 2. IA rigidity and automorphisms of a biconnected block 

7. IA automorphisms 

Throughout this section, Γ is a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices, and 

A = AΓ, χ : A −→ Z, χ(v) = 1 , H = HΓ = ker χ. 

The proof of Proposition 3.1 also shows that inclusion induces 

Hab ∼=

−−→ ker 

> (

Aab χ

−→ Z

> )

, H′ = A′. (7.1) The aim is to prove that every IA automorphism of H extends uniquely to an IA automorphism of A. The proof has two steps. First, the all-power relations on a simple circuit show that the images of its directed edge generators arise from a unique labeled polygon frame. Second, an open-ear decomposition identifies these circuit frames on their common edges and produces a global frame. Servatius blocks and period classes. For g ∈ A, let supp (g) be the set of vertices that occur in a reduced word for g; this set is independent of the chosen reduced word. Recall that g is cyclically reduced when no cyclic conjugate of a reduced word for g admits a cancellation. Assume first that g is cyclically reduced. Its noncommutation graph N (g) has vertex set supp (g), with two distinct vertices adjacent exactly when they do not commute in A, equivalently when they are not adjacent in Γ.Let B1, . . . , B k be the connected components of N (g). Vertices in distinct Bi commute, so the letters of a reduced word for g can be reordered, using commutation relations, as a product 

g = g1 · · · gk,

where supp (gi) = Bi and the factors gi commute. More canonically, if πi : A → ABi is the standard retraction, then gi = πi(g). Servatius’s block decomposition theorem says that the unordered family {g1, . . . , g k} is independent of the reduced word and uniquely determined up to permutation [43]. These nontrivial commuting factors are the Servatius blocks of g.Right-angled Artin groups have the unique-root property [ 19 ]. Hence every block has a unique expression gi = rmi 

> i

with mi ≥ 1 maximal and ri not a proper power; the ri are its 

primitive block roots . For arbitrary g, conjugate a cyclically reduced representative, form its blocks, and conjugate them back; the resulting conjugacy classes are independent of the choices. Two primitive block roots have the same period class when one is conjugate to the other or to its inverse, and the same oriented period when they are conjugate without inversion. 50 J. LEI 

7.1. Edge centralizers. For an oriented edge e = ( u, v ), put 

de = uv −1 ∈ H. (7.2) The next lemma is used both to classify edge images and to prove the uniqueness of polygon frames. 

Lemma 7.1. For every edge uv ∈ E(Γ) ,

Z(CH (de)) ∩ H′ = 1 . (7.3) 

Proof. Let Kuv = lk (u) ∩ lk (v). The cyclically reduced element de = uv −1 has the two singleton Servatius blocks u and v−1. The Servatius centralizer theorem [43] therefore gives 

CA(de) = ⟨u, v ⟩ × AKuv . (7.4) In particular, CH (de) is the height-zero subgroup of the right-hand side. We first determine its center. Let g = uavbc ∈ Z(CH (de)) , where c ∈ AKuv . For every 

y ∈ AKuv , the element yu −χ(y) belongs to CH (de). The factors u and v commute with AKuv ,and hence 1 = [ g, yu −χ(y)] = [ c, y ]. Thus c ∈ Z(AKuv ). Conversely, every height-zero element of ⟨u, v ⟩ × Z(AKuv ) centralizes the whole group CH (de). Therefore 

Z(CH (de)) = ker( χ : ⟨u, v ⟩ × Z(AKuv ) −→ Z) . (7.5) The center Z(AKuv ) is freely generated by the universal vertices of the induced graph on Kuv .Hence ⟨u, v ⟩ × Z(AKuv ) is a free abelian special subgroup, and its inclusion in Aab ∼= ZV (Γ) is injective. Proposition 3.1 gives H′ = A′, while A′ is the kernel of the abelianization map. It follows from (7.5) that an element of Z(CH (de)) ∩ H′ must be trivial. □

If α ∈ Aut (H) and he = α(de), then α carries centralizers, centers, and H′ to themselves. Thus 

Z(CH (he)) ∩ H′ = 1 . (7.6) 7.2. Period isolation. The period-matching argument uses the following theorem, proved in Appendix A. Proof. The single-letter blow-up of Lemma A.5 replaces every primitive block by a connected composite period while preserving both oriented periods and conjugacy-up-to-inversion classes. The theorem is a triviality-preserving statement, not an equality between the original and shortened power words. The orientation qualification in (ii) is essential. 7.3. Servatius decompositions of edge images. Fix α ∈ IAut (H), and, for every oriented edge e = ( u, v ), set 

he = α(de). (7.8) Under the identification (7.1), the IA condition gives

Proposition 7.3. For every oriented edge e = ( u, v ), exactly one of the following forms occurs. 

(i) Unsplit: he = re, where re is a primitive Servatius block and 

[re]ab = eu − ev.

(ii) Split: 

he = aub−1 

> v

,

where au, b v are commuting primitive Servatius blocks and 

[au]ab = eu, [bv]ab = ev.

The subscripts in the split form indicate abelianization colors; they do not assert that the blocks are vertex generators. Proof. Write the cyclic Servatius decomposition of he as 

he = rm1 

> 1

· · · rmq 

> q

, (7.10) where the rj are the actual conjugated primitive block roots. They commute, have pairwise disjoint cyclic supports, and belong to the abelian group Z(CA(he)) supplied by the Servatius centralizer theorem. No rj can have zero abelianization. Indeed, such an rj would lie in A′ = H′ and hence in H.Since it lies in Z(CA(he)) , it would belong to Z(CH (he)) ∩ H′, contradicting (7.6). Now sum the abelianizations in (7.10) . Their nonzero supports are pairwise disjoint, while their sum is the vector eu − ev by (7.9) . Therefore no coordinate outside {u, v } can occur, and there are at most two block roots. If there is one, its exponent divides both nonzero coordinates ±1 and is therefore ±1; after orienting the primitive root, one obtains the unsplit form. If there are two, their disjoint abelianization supports are respectively {u} and {v}, and their exponents again have absolute value one. Orienting both roots gives the split form. The two roots commute because they are distinct Servatius blocks of the same element. □

Lemma 7.4. Let e = ( u, v ) be an oriented edge. Suppose

Distinct oriented edges of a simple circuit have edge vectors that are not equal up to sign, and a singleton color ev is not equal up to sign to an edge vector.
