· · · b−Nm aNm = 1 . (7.15) Fix i. No primitive block of a different color can belong to the period class of ai or bi, since conjugacy preserves abelianization and inversion changes its sign. If ai and bi belonged to distinct period classes, either class would occur exactly once in (7.15) , again contradicting Theorem 7.2, clause (i). They therefore have the same period class. Their equal abelianizations rule out the inverse orientation, so they have the same oriented primitive period. This class occurs exactly twice, in the adjacent factors b−Ni aNi . Theorem 7.2, clause (ii) gives ai = bi. Set this common block equal to xCvi . The split decomposition on ei already says that xCvi commutes with xCvi+1 , so all three assertions in (7.11) follow. Uniqueness follows edgewise from Lemma 7.4. □AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 53 

7.5. Open-ear gluing. We now use biconnectedness for the first time in the extension argument. By Whitney’s open-ear decomposition theorem [51], there is a filtration 

Γ0 ⊂ Γ1 ⊂ · · · ⊂ Γs = Γ (7.16) such that Γ0 is a simple circuit and Γj = Γ j−1 ∪ Pj . Here Pj is a path whose distinct endpoints are vertices of Γj−1 and whose internal vertices are new. Ears of length one are allowed and represent edges joining two vertices already present. 

Theorem 7.6. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. For every 

α ∈ IAut (H), the circuit frames of Theorem 7.5 glue to a unique family of primitive Servatius blocks (xv)v∈V (Γ) satisfying 

[xw]ab = ew (w ∈ V (Γ)) , [xu, x v] = 1 , α(uv −1) = xux−1 

> v

(uv ∈ E(Γ)) . (7.17) 

Proof. Use the polygon frame on the initial circuit Γ0. Suppose inductively that roots satisfy-ing (7.17) have been assigned to the vertices of Γj−1. Let r, s be the endpoints of Pj , orient 

Pj from r to s, and choose a simple old path Qj from r to s inside Γj−1. Since the internal vertices of Pj are new, 

Cj = Pj Q−1 

> j

(7.18) is a simple circuit. Since Cj = Pj Q−1 

> j

is a simple circuit, its all-power relation is one of the defining Dicks–Leary relations in the full group HΓ. Theorem 7.5 supplies a frame on this circuit, with every block and equality taken in the original ambient group AΓ.Orient an edge of Qj from r toward s. The old frame and the Cj -frame give two labeled split decompositions of the same element α(uv −1). The circuit traverses this edge in the opposite direction, but inverting its displayed formula puts both decompositions in the same orientation. Lemma 7.4 then identifies the two endpoint roots. Repeating this along Qj shows that the circuit frame agrees with the old frame at every vertex of Qj , in particular at r and s. Assign the remaining circuit roots to the internal vertices of Pj . This extends (7.17) over every new vertex and edge. If Pj has length one, there is no new vertex. The same comparison along Qj identifies the circuit roots at r and s with the old ones, and the polygon formula gives α(rs −1) = xrx−1 

> s

and 

[xr, x s] = 1 on the new edge. Thus length-one ears cause no exception. Induction over (7.16) proves (7.17) on all of Γ. Edge-frame uniqueness makes the extension independent of the chosen ear decomposition; all calculations take place in HΓ ≤ AΓ.If C is any simple circuit, whether or not it was used in the chosen ear decomposition, its polygon frame and the global family give two labeled decompositions on every edge of C. The edge-frame uniqueness lemma identifies them. Thus the global family agrees with the polygon frame on every simple circuit, including circuits not used in the chosen ear decomposition. Finally, any two global families satisfying (7.17) provide, on each edge, two labeled split Servatius decompositions of the same edge image. They agree at both endpoints by the same uniqueness lemma. Connectivity then gives equality at every vertex. □

A finitely generated residually finite group is Hopfian: for each fixed index it has only finitely many subgroups of that index, and applying a surjective endomorphism to these subgroups excludes a nontrivial kernel. Thus AΓ is Hopfian. The surjective endomorphism Φ

7.8. Outer IA groups. Continue under the biconnected hypotheses of Theorem 7.10, and use restriction to identify the two IA groups. Put JA = res (Inn (A)) and JH = Inn (H). Then 

JH ≤ JA, and both are normal subgroups of the common IA group. Moreover, IOut (H) = IAut( H)/J H and IOut( A) = IAut( H)/J A.

Corollary 7.12. Under the hypotheses of Theorem 7.11, the following hold. 

(i) If Γ has a universal vertex, then χ(Z(AΓ)) = Z and IOut( HΓ) ∼= IOut( AΓ).

(ii) If Γ has no universal vertex, then Z(AΓ) = 1 and there is a central short exact sequence 

1 −→ Z −→ IOut( HΓ) −→ IOut( AΓ) −→ 1.56 J. LEI 

Proof. The center of a RAAG is generated by the universal vertices [ 43 ]. In the first case any universal vertex belongs to the center and has height one, so χ(Z(AΓ)) = Z. In the second case the center is trivial. The conclusions follow from (7.26). □

8. Automorphisms of a biconnected block 

Part 1 identifies the cohomological image up to commensurability with the unit group of an explicit order, while Section 7 identifies the kernel with the IA automorphism group of the ambient RAAG. Combining these results gives the structure theorem below and the corresponding IA consequences. Throughout, Γ is finite and biconnected with at least three vertices. 8.1. The two-step exact sequences. Since inner automorphisms act trivially on abelianiza-tion, the cohomology representation descends from Aut (HΓ) to Out (HΓ) and has the same image ΛΓ.

Theorem 8.1. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. There are short exact sequences 

1 −→ IAut( AΓ) −→ Aut( HΓ) ρW

−−→ ΛΓ −→ 1 (8.1)

> Γ

1 −→ AΓ

HΓZ(AΓ) −→ IOut( HΓ) −→ IOut( AΓ) −→ 1 (8.3) 

is central. Proof. The kernel of ρW is IAut (HΓ) by definition, and Theorem 7.10 identifies this group with 

IAut (AΓ) by restriction. This proves (8.1) . Quotienting by Inn (HΓ) gives (8.2) . The common finite-index assertion is Theorem 6.19, and (8.3) is Theorem 7.11. □

The theorem separates the two contributions. Restrictions of ambient partial conjugations and commutator transvections generate the IA kernel. Sector shears occur in the cohomological quotient and, together with the ambient root automorphisms, generate a finite-index subgroup of its arithmetic derived part. 8.2. Finite generation. Corollary 8.2. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. Both Aut (HΓ)

and Out( HΓ) are finitely generated. Proof. Day’s theorem makes IAut (AΓ) finitely generated [ 16 , Theorem B], while Corollary 6.20 makes ΛΓ finitely generated. The first exact sequence in Theorem 8.1 now proves finite generation of Aut (HΓ). Its quotient Out (HΓ) is finitely generated as well. Equivalently, one may use the second sequence together with the central outer IA sequence and finite generation of IOut( AΓ). □AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 57

Remark 8.5 (Existence versus effectivity) . The finite root-generating family exists by finite generation of EΓ. No algorithm or uniform bound for choosing such a family is claimed. 8.5. Residual nilpotence of IAut and IOut . Wade’s Andreadakis–Johnson filtration is separating and has torsion-free abelian successive quotients, both before and after passing to outer automorphisms [ 49 , Lemma 4.4, Theorem 4.6, Proposition 4.7 and Theorem 4.9]. The restriction isomorphism gives the same conclusion for IAut (HΓ). The central cyclic discrepancy in the outer group can also be incorporated into the same filtration. 

Proposition 8.6. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. Each of 

IAut( AΓ), IAut( HΓ), IOut( AΓ), IOut( HΓ)

is residually torsion-free nilpotent. Therefore all four groups are torsion-free and bi-orderable; because they are finitely generated, they are residually p for every prime p.Proof. The statements for the two ambient RAAG groups are Wade’s theorems [ 49 , Lemma 4.4, Theorem 4.6, Proposition 4.7 and Theorem 4.9]; the outer assertion was also proved inde-pendently by Toinet [ 48 , Theorem 1.6]. IAut( HΓ).It remains only to treat K = IOut (HΓ) when Γ has no universal vertex; in the universal-vertex case K ∼= IOut (AΓ) by Corollary 7.12. Let (Gc)c≥1 be Wade’s Johnson filtration of

The stated consequences follow from the standard residual properties of torsion-free nilpotent groups; see, for example, Toinet’s discussion of residual p-finiteness and bi-orderability [ 48 ]. In particular, finitely generated torsion-free nilpotent groups are bi-orderable and residually p for every prime. For any nontrivial element, first choose a torsion-free nilpotent quotient detecting it and then a finite p-group quotient detecting its image. □

8.6. The Andreadakis–Johnson filtration. The preceding subsection used Wade’s filtration through the restriction isomorphism. We now compare it with the intrinsic filtration of 

IAut (HΓ): restriction identifies the intrinsic Andreadakis–Johnson filtrations of IAut (AΓ) and

(8.12) By Duchamp–Krob, the lower-central quotients of A are free abelian, and gr γ (A) ⊗ Q is the partially commutative graph Lie algebra LΓ on the vertex generators, embedded in the partially commutative tensor algebra spanned by the trace monomials [ 19 ]; both are graded by vertex multidegrees. We first prove two centralizer statements in LΓ.

Lemma 8.7. Let g ∈ LΓ be multihomogeneous of multidegree δ with total degree |δ| ≥ 2, and let x be a vertex with δx ≥ 1. If g̸ = 0 , then [g, x ]̸ = 0 .Proof. Suppose [g, x ] = 0 , that is, gx = xg in the tensor algebra. Write g = ∑ 

> t

ctt over the finitely many trace monomials t of multidegree δ with ct̸ = 0 . The trace monoid is cancellative [ 11 ], so t 7 → tx and t 7 → xt are injective on monomials; comparing the two expansions of gx = xg therefore produces a bijection σ of supp( g) with 

tx = x σ (t), cσ(t) = ct.

Iterating gives t x k = xk σk(t) for all k ≥ 0, and each σk(t) again has multidegree δ.Assume some t ∈ supp (g) contains a letter c / ∈ st (x). Deleting all letters other than x and c

is an algebra homomorphism onto the trace algebra of the induced subgraph on {x, c }; since c

and x do not commute, the target is the free associative algebra on x, c . Apply the deletion to 

t x k = xk σk(t) with k = δx + 1 . On the left, the image of t is a word containing δc ≥ 1 letters 

c among at most δx letters x, so its first c occurs within the first δx + 1 letters. On the right, 60 J. LEI 

the word begins with xδx+1 , so its first c occurs strictly later. Equal words have equal first-c

positions, a contradiction. Hence every monomial of g has all its letters in st( x).Now consider instead the algebra homomorphism onto the trace algebra of Γ[ st (x)] that sends every generator outside st (x) to zero and fixes the others; it is well defined because the defining commutation relations map to relations or to 0 = 0 . It fixes g, whose monomials use only letters of st (x), and it maps Lie generators to Lie generators or to zero, hence maps LΓ

onto LΓ[st( x)] ; so g ∈ LΓ[st( x)] . The induced subgraph Γ[ st (x)] is the join of {x} with Γ[ lk (x)] . In the graph Lie algebra of a join the two sides commute: a centralizer of a subset is a subalgebra, and each generator on one side commutes with each generator on the other. Hence 

LΓ[st( x)] = Qx ⊕ LΓ[lk( x)] ,

the sum being direct by multidegree. The multidegree-δ component of the right-hand side vanishes: Qx is concentrated in multidegree ex with |ex| = 1 < |δ|, while every multidegree occurring in LΓ[lk( x)] has x-entry zero. Thus g = 0 , a contradiction. □

Lemma 8.9. Let Φ ∈ J r(AΓ) with r ≥ 1 and let uv ∈ E(Γ) . Then, in gr r+2 (AΓ),

[τr(Φ)( eu), e v] + [ eu, τ r(Φ)( ev)] = 0 .

Proof. Put a = Φ( u)u−1 and b = Φ( v)v−1, both in γr+1 A. Applying Φ to the edge relation 

[u, v ] = 1 gives [au, bv ] = 1 . Collect commutators modulo γr+3 A: the term [a, b ] lies in 

[γr+1 A, γ r+1 A] ≤ γ2r+2 A ≤ γr+3 A, and conjugating an element of γr+2 A changes it only by an element of [γr+2 A, A ] ≤ γr+3 A. The standard commutator expansions of [au, bv ] therefore leave 

1 ≡ [a, v ] [ u, b ] (mod γr+3 A),

the factor [u, v ] = 1 having been deleted. Reading this additively in gr r+2 (AΓ) gives the assertion. □

Proposition 8.10. Let Γ be connected with at least two vertices, let r ≥ 1, and let Φ ∈ J r(AΓ).If τr(Φ) vanishes on LZ, then τr(Φ) = 0 ; equivalently, Φ ∈ J r+1 (AΓ).Proof. Since eu − ev ∈ LZ for all vertices u, v , the hypothesis gives a single class z ∈ gr r+1 (A)

with τr(Φ)( ev) = z for every v, that is, τr(Φ) = ε(·) z. For every edge uv , Lemma 8.9 yields 

0 = [ z, e v] + [ eu, z ] = [ z, e v − eu]AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 61 

in gr r+2 (A). Because Γ is connected, the edge differences ev − eu span LQ; hence [z ⊗ 1, L Q] = 0 

in LΓ, with z ⊗ 1 homogeneous of degree r + 1 ≥ 2. Lemma 8.8 gives z ⊗ 1 = 0 , and since 

gr r+1 (A) is free abelian [19], z = 0 . Now (8.12) applies. □

Theorem 8.11. Let Γ be a finite connected graph with at least two vertices. 

(i) For every k ≥ 1 and every Φ ∈ IAut( AΓ),

8.7. Cycle groups. For cycle graphs with at least five vertices, the Bestvina–Brady group is not finitely presented, whereas its outer automorphism group is virtually infinite cyclic. The proof computes the outer IA kernel and the cohomological quotient separately. 

Theorem 8.12. For every n ≥ 5,

HCn is not finitely presented , Out( HCn ) is virtually infinite cyclic .

Failure of finite presentation. Write ∆Γ for the flag complex of a graph Γ. The Bestvina–Brady finite-presentation criterion [3] says that, for connected Γ,

HΓ is finitely presented ⇐⇒ ∆Γ is simply connected . (8.13) When Γ = Cn with n ≥ 5, the flag complex is the one-dimensional cycle itself. Hence 

HCn is not finitely presented . (8.14) 62 J. LEI 

The outer IA group. Day’s generating theorem [ 16 , Theorem B] generates IAut (ACn ) by partial conjugations and admissible commutator transvections. For every vertex v, the graph Cn − st (v) is a path on n − 3 vertices and is therefore connected. Thus there is only one nontrivial component available for a partial conjugation with multiplier 

v. That automorphism conjugates every vertex outside st (v) by v and fixes st (v). Since v

commutes with all vertices in its star, this partial conjugation is conjugation by v or by v−1,according to the chosen convention. There is also no domination between distinct cycle vertices. Indeed, if x̸ = y, the two neighbors of x are not both contained in st (y) when n ≥ 5. If x and y are adjacent, the neighbor of x on the other side is omitted from st (y); if they are nonadjacent, the only cycle in which y can be adjacent to both neighbors of x is C4. Hence lk (x)̸ ⊆ st (y) whenever x̸ = y and 

n ≥ 5. An admissible commutator transvection requires the moved vertex to be dominated by its multipliers, so none exists. Every IA automorphism of ACn is therefore inner: 

IOut( ACn ) = 1 . (8.15) The graph Cn is biconnected and has no universal vertex. The outer IA sequence (7.25) ,together with (8.15), becomes 

1 −→ ACn /H Cn −→ IOut( HCn ) −→ 1.

Since ACn /H Cn ∼= Z via the height character, 

IOut( HCn ) ∼= Z. (8.16) The generator is the outer class of deck conjugation by any height-one vertex. The injection in the outer IA sequence proves directly that this class has infinite order; no splitting assertion is involved. The cohomological image. It remains to show that the cohomological quotient is finite. Recall that LCn = ker

Lemma 8.13. For n ≥ 5, the inclusion-minimal vertex separators of Cn are exactly the nonadjacent pairs. Proof. Deleting one vertex leaves a path, so no singleton separates. Deleting two adjacent vertices also leaves a path, whereas deleting two nonadjacent vertices leaves two nonempty path components. Finally, every set of at least three cycle vertices contains a nonadjacent pair and therefore cannot be inclusion-minimal. □

Lemma 8.14. The stabilizer of Dn in GL( LCn ) is finite. Proof. The complement graph Cn is connected for every n ≥ 5; for n = 5 it is another five-cycle. Choose a spanning tree T ⊆ Cn. Its n − 1 oriented edge vectors 

ei − ej (ij ∈ E(T )) (8.18) form a Z-basis of LCn . On the tree basis (8.18) ,an element of the kernel is therefore determined by at most 2n−1 sign choices. Thus both the kernel and the full stabilizer are finite. □

1 −→ IOut( HCn ) −→ Out( HCn ) −→ ΛCn −→ 1.

Equations (8.16) and (8.19) show that Out (HCn ) contains an infinite cyclic subgroup of finite index. Together with (8.14), this proves Theorem 8.12. 

Remark 8.15 (Why C4 is different) . The bound n ≥ 5 is structural rather than cosmetic. The four-cycle is K2,2, its two opposite vertices dominate one another in the relevant directions, and both its RAAG automorphism group and its cohomological image have nontrivial linear blocks. Thus the arguments proving a cyclic outer IA kernel and a finite cohomological quotient for 

Cn, n ≥ 5, do not apply to C4.

Part 3. Global structure, rigidity, and sharpness 

9. Blocks and the Grushko decomposition 

We now pass from biconnected defining graphs to arbitrary finite connected graphs. There are two points to prove. First, the Dicks–Leary presentation splits along the graph blocks. Second, the factors attached to biconnected blocks admit no further free splitting. Together these statements identify the graph-block decomposition with the Grushko decomposition of HΓ.The block free-product decomposition already appears in Chang–Ruffoni [ 12 , Cor. 4.21–4.22], and their one-endedness criterion [ 12 , Cor. 4.21] gives the second point when HΓ is finitely presented. Our contribution is to read free indecomposability off the intrinsic BNS invariant of Part 1, which carries no finiteness hypothesis and so covers every finite connected Γ.9.1. The block free product. Recall that B2(Γ) denotes the blocks with at least three vertices and that b(Γ) is the number of bridges. 

Proposition 9.1. For every finite connected graph Γ, there is a natural free-product decompo-sition 

HΓ ∼=

> (

∗B∈B 2(Γ) HB

> )

∗ Fb(Γ) . (9.1) 

Proof. Every edge of Γ belongs to exactly one graph block, and every simple circuit is contained in a unique biconnected block. Apply the Dicks–Leary presentation from Theorem 2.1. Its generators therefore split into disjoint sets indexed by the blocks. It remains to see that its relations split in the same way. A closed graph path having a repeated vertex decomposes as a concatenation of shorter closed paths. If C = C1C2 is such a concatenation and Wn(C) denotes the all-power word, then Wn(C) = Wn(C1)Wn(C2). A backtrack e¯e contributes ene−n. Induction on path length thus expresses every closed-path relation as a product of relations carried by simple circuits and inverse-edge relations. Each of these is contained in one block. Conversely, a Dicks–Leary relation for a block is also a relation 64 J. LEI 

for HΓ. The resulting presentation is Therefore the disjoint union of the presentations for the block groups HB , and hence presents their free product. If uv is a bridge of Γ, the corresponding block B has V (B) = {u, v }. For this block, AB ∼= Z2

and 

HB = ker

= ⟨uv −1⟩ ∼= Z.

Thus the b(Γ) bridge blocks contribute b(Γ) infinite cyclic free factors, whose free product is 

Fb(Γ) . This proves (9.1). □

9.2. Biconnected blocks give freely indecomposable factors. When the flag complex 

∆Γ is simply connected—equivalently, when HΓ is finitely presented—the equivalence of biconnectedness and one-endedness is due to Chang–Ruffoni [ 12 , Cor. 4.21], whose criterion runs through the Bieri–Neumann–Strebel invariant Σ1(HΓ) and is stated under that finiteness hypothesis. The intrinsic computation of Σ1(HΓ) in Part 1 removes the hypothesis: Theorem 3.6 holds for every finite connected graph, including graphs for which HΓ is not finitely presented, such as the four-cycle C4 [12 , Example 4.18], where HC4 = ker (F2 × F2 → Z) is finitely generated but not finitely presented. 

Proposition 9.2. If B is a biconnected graph with at least three vertices, then HB is freely indecomposable and is not infinite cyclic. Proof. Because B has no cut vertex, every vertex separator of B has at least two vertices, so each subspace WS, R appearing in the arrangement of Theorem 3.6 is a proper subspace of the character space. That theorem identifies Σ1(HB )c with the union of these finitely many proper subspaces. A real vector space is never the union of finitely many proper subspaces, so some character avoids them all and Σ1(HB )̸ = ∅. Suppose HB ∼= G1 ∗ G2 were a nontrivial free product. Since B is connected, HB is finitely generated, and hence so are G1 and G2. The Bieri–Neumann–Strebel invariant of a nontrivial free product of finitely generated groups is empty [9], contradicting Σ1(HB )̸ = ∅. Therefore HB is freely indecomposable. Finally, rank Hab  

> B

= |V (B)| − 1 ≥ 2 by Proposition 2.2, so HB is not infinite cyclic. □

Theorem 9.3 (Grushko decomposition) . Let Γ be a finite connected graph. Then 

HΓ ∼=

> (

∗B∈B 2(Γ) HB

> )

∗ Fb(Γ) (9.2) 

is its Grushko decomposition: every factor HB displayed on the right is nontrivial, noncyclic, and freely indecomposable, and Fb(Γ) is the free part. Proof. Proposition 9.1 gives the decomposition, and Proposition 9.2 gives the required prop-erties of the nonfree factors. The uniqueness part of Grushko’s theorem [ 23 , 44 ] therefore identifies (9.2) as the Grushko decomposition of HΓ. □

Theorem 9.3 reduces the global automorphism problem to the standard automorphism theory of the displayed finite free product; the block factors admit no further free decomposition. 

Corollary 9.4. Let Γ be a finite connected graph with at least two vertices. Exactly one of the following holds. 

(i) Γ = K2: then HΓ ∼= Z has two ends. 

(ii) Γ is biconnected with at least three vertices: then HΓ is one-ended. 

(iii) Γ has a cut vertex: then HΓ has infinitely many ends. In particular, for |V (Γ) | ≥ 3, the group HΓ is one-ended if and only if Γ is biconnected. AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 65 

Proof. The group HΓ is finitely generated because Γ is connected, and it is torsion-free as a subgroup of the torsion-free group AΓ. A finitely generated group has 0, 1, 2, or infinitely many ends; it has 0 ends exactly when it is finite, and a two-ended torsion-free group is infinite cyclic [ 21 , Ch. 13]. By Stallings’s structure theorem, a finitely generated torsion-free group with infinitely many ends splits over the trivial subgroup and is therefore either infinite cyclic or a nontrivial free product [45]. Case (i) is immediate. In case (ii), HΓ is infinite, is not infinite cyclic, and is freely indecomposable (Proposition 9.2); by the preceding paragraph it can have neither two nor infinitely many ends, so it is one-ended. In case (iii), the graph has at least two blocks, so the Grushko decomposition (9.2) has at least two free factors and HΓ = P ∗ Q with P, Q nontrivial and torsion-free, hence infinite. A nontrivial free product of two infinite groups has infinitely many ends [21, Ch. 13]. □

10. The global automorphism structure 

Let Γ be finite and connected. Write its Grushko decomposition from Theorem 9.3 as 

HΓ = G1 ∗ · · · ∗ Gs ∗ Fq, Gi = HBi , q = b(Γ) . (10.1) The groups Gi are the noncyclic freely indecomposable factors. We first construct a finite wreath-product quotient whose coordinates encode permutations of isomorphic block factors and the finite blockwise cohomological quotients. Its kernel admits a pure restriction sequence. Combining that sequence with the arithmetic–IA structure of the individual blocks yields a finite subnormal series and a finite generating set for a finite-index subgroup. 10.1. Intrinsic finite-index subgroups of the block factors. For each i, let ¯ρi :Out (Gi) −→ Λi = Λ Bi be the surjective representation onto the image on H1(Gi; Z). Let

f P if −1 = Pj (10.3) 

under the induced isomorphism of outer automorphism groups. Proof. The actual cohomological image preserves I2 and I3 and permutes the finite BNS separator arrangement. Therefore it normalizes the subgroup that fixes every separator space: conjugation permutes the defining pointwise-stabilizer conditions. It therefore normalizes the combined stabilizer from (3.27) , its identity component, and its Lie algebra. By (6.1) ,that Lie algebra is the cubic component algebra CBi . Since the action also preserves the integral cohomology lattice, it normalizes the order OBi = CBi ∩ End (H1(Gi; Z)) . Conjugation preserves rank and square-zero, so the actual image permutes all integral rank-one square-zero roots. Hence Ei ◁ Λi and Pi ◁ Out (Gi). The common-index theorem 6.19 gives [Λ i : Ei] < ∞,and therefore Pi has finite index. An abstract group isomorphism transports the lower-central relation spaces, the BNS invariant, and the integral cohomology lattice. It Therefore transports the combined stabilizer, its identity-component algebra, its order, and the family of integral rank-one square-zero roots. Thus it carries Ei to Ej , and taking preimages proves (10.3). □66 J. LEI

10.3. The pure restriction sequence. Let Out 0(HΓ; [ G1], . . . , [Gs]) be the subgroup fixing the conjugacy class of every nonfree factor. Normalized restriction defines a surjection to ∏ 

> i

Out (Gi): representatives of factor automorphisms extend by the identity on the remaining free factors. Following the relative free-product automorphism terminology of Gilbert [ 22 ,Section 1 and Theorem 2.20] and the outer-space framework of Guirardel–Levitt [ 25 ], we denote the full kernel of this restriction map by FR (G1, . . . , G s; Fq). Our notation includes the automorphisms of the free factor and the mixed relative Whitehead moves; in some conventions, “Fouxe–Rabinovitch group” denotes a smaller subgroup. 

Lemma 10.4. Let G = G1 ∗ · · · ∗ Gs ∗ Fq, where every Gi is finitely generated. Choose a finite symmetric generating set Si for each Gi and a free basis X = {x1, . . . , x q} for Fq. Then 

FR( G1, . . . , G s; Fq) is generated by the following finite families: 

(R1) for i̸ = j and g ∈ Sj , conjugate the whole factor Gi by g and fix all other displayed generators; also conjugate Gi by each xr;

(R2) a finite Nielsen generating set of Aut (Fq), consisting of permutations, inversions, and elementary Nielsen transvections; 

(R3) for g ∈ Si and every r, the two mixed moves 

xr 7 −→ gx r, xr 7 −→ xrg, 

fixing all other factors and free generators. Only finite generation of the factors is used; no finite presentation of any Gi is required. Proof. We use only the generating assertion in Gilbert’s relative Whitehead theorem [ 22 ,Theorem 2.20 and Section 1]. In Gilbert’s full generating list, factor automorphisms map to the restriction quotient and permutations map to the finite permutation quotient. After passing to the kernel of normalized restriction and then to outer automorphisms, the remaining relative Whitehead generators have precisely the following forms: a nonfree factor is conjugated by a multiplier from another factor or by a free letter, giving (R1); the generators supported entirely on the free factor are the usual Nielsen generators in (R2); and a free letter is multiplied on the left or right by a factor element, giving (R3). Equivalently, in the terminology of the present lemma, Gilbert’s factor-conjugating generators give (R1), his generators supported on the free factor give the Nielsen family (R2), and his left- and right-multiplier generators give (R3). Thus no presentation theorem of Gilbert is being used here. Gilbert allows the factor multipliers to range over all elements of the factors. Every such multiplier is a word in the chosen finite set Si. Partial conjugations with fixed target compose according to multiplication in the multiplier factor, and left and right mixed transvections decompose along that word. Hence the displayed finite families generate the corresponding moves with arbitrary factor multipliers. It remains to express an inner automorphism cg of one factor Gi, extended by the identity on all other factors. Compose it with the inverse global inner automorphism c−1 

> g

. The resulting outer representative conjugates every other nonfree factor by g−1 and sends each free letter to 

g−1xrg; these are products of the operations in (R1) and the two operations in (R3). Thus centers of factors require no additional generator. This proves the asserted finite generating list modulo global inner automorphisms. □

Theorem 10.7. For every finite connected graph Γ, the normal finite-index subgroup Out †(HΓ)

has a finite subnormal series whose successive quotients are drawn from the following list:

(iii) trivial or infinite cyclic central groups; 

(iv) finitely generated torsion-free nilpotent groups; 

(v) arithmetic subgroups of split products ∏ SL m(Q), commensurable with the corresponding products of integral special linear groups. The quotient Out( HΓ)/ Out †(HΓ) is the finite wreath product displayed in (10.9) .

10.6. Finite generation and generators. Theorem 10.8. If Γ is finite and connected, then both Aut (HΓ) and Out (HΓ) are finitely generated. Proof. The kernel in (10.10) is finitely generated by Theorem 10.5. Every Pi is finitely generated: its kernel IOut (Gi) is finitely generated by the outer IA sequence and Day’s theorem [ 16 ,Theorem B], while its quotient Ei is finitely generated by the proof of Corollary 6.20. Thus 

Out †(HΓ) is finitely generated. The wreath quotient is finite, so Out (HΓ) is finitely generated. Finally, 

1 −→ HΓ/Z (HΓ) −→ Aut( HΓ) −→ Out( HΓ) −→ 1

is exact. The inner kernel is finitely generated because HΓ is, and the extension proves finite generation of Aut( HΓ). □

A finite generating set for Out †(HΓ) may be chosen from the following families: (G1) on each block, restrictions of the normalized IA partial conjugations and commutator transvections; (G2) on each block, lifts of a finite generating set of Ei; every defining root has one of the ambient or sector-shear formulas proved in Section 5; (G3) the relative operations (R1)–(R3) in the proof of Theorem 10.5. Adding representatives for generators of the finite wreath quotient gives a finite generating set for the full outer group; adding finitely many inner automorphisms gives one for the full automorphism group. 11. The Tits alternative relative to virtually polycyclic groups 

We now pass from the structure of the full automorphism groups to the structure of their arbitrary subgroups. For a group G, write TA vpc (G) for the assertion that every subgroup of 

G either contains a nonabelian free group or is virtually polycyclic. This is the Tits alternative relative to the class of virtually polycyclic groups; it is the property called the strong Tits alternative in this paper. We first prove an extension lemma, then apply the arithmetic–IA decomposition to a biconnected block, and finally apply Horbez’s theorem [ 24 ] to the Grushko decomposition from Section 9. 70 J. LEI 

11.1. An extension principle. If the image of a subgroup under an epimorphism contains a nonabelian free group, projectivity of free groups lifts such a subgroup to the domain. 

Lemma 11.1. Let p : G ↠ Q be an epimorphism and let K ≤ G. If p(K) contains a subgroup isomorphic to F2, then K contains a subgroup isomorphic to F2.Proof. Choose F ≤ p(K) with F ∼= F2 and put P = K ∩ p−1(F ). The restriction P ↠ F is onto. Lifts in P of a free basis of F determine, by the universal property, a section F → P .Thus F embeds in K. □

Lemma 11.2. Let N , G, and Q be groups fitting into a short exact sequence 

1 −→ N −→ G p

−→ Q −→ 1. (11.1) 

If N and Q satisfy TA vpc , then so does G.Proof. Let K ≤ G contain no F2. The subgroup K ∩ N is virtually polycyclic. Lemma 11.1 shows that p(K) contains no F2, so p(K) is virtually polycyclic as well. The sequence 

1 −→ K ∩ N −→ K −→ p(K) −→ 1

and closure of virtually polycyclic groups under extensions [ 39 , Ch. I] imply that K is virtually polycyclic. □

Virtually polycyclic groups are closed under subgroups, extensions, and passage to finite-index supergroups [ 40 , Chapter 1]. It follows from the definition that TA vpc is inherited by subgroups and is invariant under passage between finite-index subgroups and finite-index supergroups. 11.2. Auxiliary Tits alternatives. We use the following external Tits theorems. 

Proposition 11.3. For every finite graph ∆ and every n ≥ 1:

(i) A∆ and all its subgroups satisfy TA vpc ;

(ii) Out( A∆) satisfies TA vpc ;

(iii) every subgroup of GL n(Z) satisfies TA vpc ;

(iv) Aut( A∆) and its subgroup IAut( A∆) satisfy TA vpc .Proof. Statement (i) is the RAAG specialization of the graph-product theorem of Antolín– Minasyan [ 2, Theorem 4.3]. Statement (ii) is Sale–Susse’s relative Tits alternative for outer automorphism groups of graph products of finitely generated abelian groups [ 38 , Corollary 4.4]. For (iii), the classical Tits alternative in characteristic zero says that a linear subgroup containing no F2 is virtually solvable [ 47 ]. Mal’cev’s theorem makes every solvable subgroup of 

GL n(Z) polycyclic [33]; hence the original subgroup is virtually polycyclic. For (iv), let U be the set of universal vertices of ∆. Then 

A∆ = A∆−U × ZU , Inn( A∆) = A∆/Z (A∆) ∼= A∆−U .

The kernel and quotient in 

1 −→ Inn( A∆) −→ Aut( A∆) −→ Out( A∆) −→ 1

satisfy the alternative by (i) and (ii). Apply Lemma 11.2, and then use heredity for IAut (A∆).

□AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 71

and Out( HB ) satisfy TA vpc .Proof. Proposition 11.3 gives the alternative for IAut (AB ) and for the integral linear group

The argument only uses that the cohomological image is integral linear. The stronger arithmetic description from Section 6 determines whether the entire outer automorphism group is virtually polycyclic. 11.4. Arbitrary connected graphs. The following is Horbez’s free-product theorem; the peripheral family may be empty, which includes the free-group case [24, Theorem 6.1]. 

Theorem 11.5 (Horbez’s free-product theorem) . Let G = G1 ∗ · · · ∗ Gs ∗ Fq, where the Gi are countable, freely indecomposable, and not isomorphic to Z, and Fq is finitely generated free. Let C be a class of groups stable under isomorphisms, subgroups, extensions, and passage to finite-index supergroups, and assume Z ∈ C . If every Gi and every Out (Gi) satisfies the Tits alternative relative to C, then Out( G) satisfies that relative alternative. 

Let Γ be a finite connected graph. By Theorem 9.3, 

HΓ = HB1 ∗ · · · ∗ HBs ∗ Fq, (11.5) where every HBi is noncyclic and freely indecomposable. Each factor HBi is a subgroup of the RAAG ABi , so it satisfies TA vpc by Proposition 11.3; its outer automorphism group satisfies the same alternative by Proposition 11.4. The class of virtually polycyclic groups satisfies all closure hypotheses in Theorem 11.5. That theorem therefore applies to (11.5) and gives the outer conclusion. To obtain the full automorphism group without appealing to a quotient principle, we identify the inner kernel. The centralizer identity (7.20) gives Z(HΓ) = HΓ ∩Z(AΓ). Therefore inclusion induces 

Inn( HΓ) = HΓ/Z (HΓ) ↪→ AΓ/Z (AΓ). (11.6) Deleting the universal vertices of Γ identifies the group on the right with a RAAG. Thus 

Inn( HΓ) satisfies TA vpc . Lemma 11.2 applied to 

1 −→ Inn( HΓ) −→ Aut( HΓ) −→ Out( HΓ) −→ 1

then gives the automorphism conclusion. 

Theorem 11.6 (Tits alternative relative to virtually polycyclic groups) . Let Γ be a finite connected graph. Every subgroup of Aut (HΓ) or Out (HΓ) either is virtually polycyclic or contains a subgroup isomorphic to F2.72 J. LEI 

Proof. The preceding application of Horbez’s theorem proves the assertion for Out (HΓ), and the inner-kernel argument proves it for Aut (HΓ). If Γ has one vertex or one edge, then HΓ

is respectively trivial or infinite cyclic and the assertion is immediate. If the decomposition has no nonfree factor, then HΓ = Fq and Horbez’s theorem applies with an empty peripheral family. □

11.5. A criterion for virtual polycyclicity. We now return to a biconnected graph Γ.A separating intersection of links (SIL) is a triple (x, y | z) such that x and y are distinct nonadjacent vertices and z lies in a component of Γ − (lk (x) ∩ lk (y)) that contains neither x

nor y [14, Definition 3.2]. 

Proposition 11.7. For every finite graph Γ,

Γ has no SIL ⇐⇒ IOut( AΓ) is finitely generated abelian .

If Γ has a SIL, then F2 ≤ IOut( AΓ).Proof. Day’s theorem gives the partial conjugations and commutator transvections generating 

IAut (AΓ) [ 16 , Theorem B]. Passing to outer classes generates IOut (AΓ) because inner automor-phisms act trivially on abelianization. If there is no SIL, no nontrivial commutator transvection survives in the outer group: indeed, the domination conditions for a commutator transvection with target v and nonadjacent multipliers x, y imply lk (v) ⊆ st (x) ∩ st (y). The target is adjacent to neither multiplier: if, for example, v were adjacent to x, the domination v ≼ y

would force x ∈ st (y), contrary to the distinct nonadjacent choice of x, y . The same applies to y. Hence lk (v) ⊆ lk (x) ∩ lk (y), and {v} is a component of the complement of the common link, so (x, y | v) is a SIL. Partial conjugations with the same multiplier commute because their component supports are equal or disjoint. Those with distinct multipliers commute in the outer group in the absence of a SIL [ 38 , Lemma 1.7]. Hence the finite generating set is pairwise commuting. Conversely, let (x, y | z) be a SIL and let C be the component of the complement of the common link that contains z. The partial conjugations Px,C and Py,C are defined and lie in the IA subgroup. Indeed, a vertex of C adjacent to x would connect C to the surviving vertex 

x in the complement of the common link, and similarly for y. Thus C meets neither link. Connectivity and maximality then show that C is a component of both Γ − st (x) and Γ − st (y).Retract to the special subgroup A{x,y,z } = F (x, y, z ). A reduced word w in the two partial conjugations fixes x, y and sends z 7 −→ w(x, y )zw (x, y )−1. If this automorphism were conjugation by g ∈ AΓ, the canonical retraction r : AΓ → F (x, y, z ) would make its restricted action conjugation by r(g). Since the action fixes x, y , one has r(g) ∈ CF (x) ∩ CF (y) = 1 by the free-group centralizer theorem [ 32 , Ch. I]. The action on z is therefore trivial, and the same theorem gives w ∈ CF (z) ∩ F (x, y ) = 1 . Thus the two outer classes freely generate F2. □

Proof. Theorem 6.19 gives a common finite-index root subgroup of ΛΓ and O×

For any finite-dimensional unital algebra A with J = Jac (A), the ideal A[A, A ]A is nilpotent exactly when A/J is commutative. Indeed, commutativity modulo J puts this ideal inside the nilpotent ideal J; conversely every nilpotent two-sided ideal lies in J [31 ], so its containing all commutators forces A/J to be commutative. Applying this to A = CΓ and using (11.7) shows that the quotient is commutative exactly when all mr equal 1. This proves (ii) ⇔(iii). □

Theorem 11.9. Let Γ be finite and biconnected with at least three vertices. The following are equivalent: 

(i) Out( HΓ) is virtually polycyclic; 

(ii) Γ has no SIL and every Wedderburn factor in (11.7) is one-dimensional; 

(iii) Γ has no SIL and cΓ is nilpotent. If these conditions fail, then F2 ≤ Out( HΓ).Proof. The exact sequences (11.3) and (11.4) , together with closure of virtually polycyclic groups under subgroups, quotients, and extensions, give 

Out( HΓ) virtually polycyclic ⇐⇒ 

> {

IOut( AΓ) virtually polycyclic ,

Proposition 11.7 and Lemma 11.8 prove the equivalences. If they fail, the full outer group is not virtually polycyclic; hence Theorem 11.6 then implies that it contains a subgroup isomorphic to F2. □

These conditions are decidable from Γ. SILs are determined by finitely many component computations, the matrices defining CΓ are obtained from the cubic component hypergraphs of Section 4, and nilpotence of cΓ is decidable by exact rational linear algebra. Thus virtual polycyclicity is decidable by finite graph computations and rational linear algebra; it is not characterized here by a finite list of forbidden induced subgraphs. 11.6. Subgroup consequences. Corollary 11.10. Let G be either Aut( HΓ) or Out( HΓ) for a finite connected graph Γ.

(i) every amenable, virtually solvable, nilpotent, or abelian subgroup of G is virtually polycyclic and hence finitely generated; 

(ii) every torsion subgroup of G is finite, and therefore every locally finite subgroup is finite. Proof. An amenable group cannot contain F2. Neither can a virtually solvable group: intersect-ing a hypothetical F2 with a finite-index solvable subgroup would put a finite-index nonabelian free group inside a solvable group. Theorem 11.6 proves (i). A torsion group contains no F2, so it is virtually polycyclic; a torsion virtually polycyclic group is finite [ 39 , Ch. I]. Every locally finite group is torsion, giving (ii). □

In particular, neither automorphism group contains (Q, +) , a Prüfer p-group, an infinite locally finite group, a lamplighter group, or BS (1 , n ) for |n| ≥ 2: all are amenable, while none is virtually polycyclic. 74 J. LEI 

12. Torsion and virtual cohomological dimension 

This section extracts two different consequences of the block structure. First, for biconnected 

Γ, finite-order automorphisms of HΓ are detected faithfully by the cohomological representation. Second, the arithmetic description gives an exact virtual-cohomological-dimension formula for the cohomological image. Combining that formula with IA rigidity proves finite virtual cohomological dimension for the full automorphism groups of a biconnected block. Throughout the first four subsections, Γ is finite and biconnected with at least three vertices. Set nΓ = rank H1(HΓ; Z) = |V (Γ) | − 1.12.1. Finite-order detection and the level-three kernel. The torsion-freeness of the two IA subgroups from Proposition 8.6 immediately makes the cohomological representation faithful on every finite subgroup. 

Proposition 12.1. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. For every finite subgroup F ≤ Aut( HΓ), the cohomological representation restricts to an injection 

F ↪ → GL nΓ (Z).

The same conclusion holds for every finite subgroup F ≤ Out( HΓ).Proof. The kernel of the representation of Aut (HΓ) is IAut (HΓ), which is torsion-free. A finite subgroup therefore has trivial intersection with the kernel. The outer kernel is IOut (HΓ) and is torsion-free by the same proposition, so the identical argument applies. □

The following is the classical level-three case of Minkowski’s congruence lemma [ 41 , Ch. VII]. We include the elementary proof because it identifies a concrete torsion-free finite-index subgroup. 

Lemma 12.2 (Minkowski’s level-three lemma) . Let M be a free abelian group of finite rank. The principal congruence subgroup 

ΓM (3) = ker(GL( M ) −→ GL( M/ 3M )) 

is torsion-free. Proof. Choose a basis of the lattice and suppose Q̸ = I has finite order in ΓM (3) . Replacing Q

by a power, assume it has prime order q. Choose the largest k ≥ 1 for which Q = I + 3 kA

with A integral and not every entry of A divisible by 3. If q̸ = 3 , expand Qq = I, divide by 3k,and reduce modulo 3. Every term of degree at least two vanishes, leaving qA ≡ 0 ( mod 3) , a contradiction. If q = 3 , then 

(I + 3 kA)3 = I + 3 k+1 A + 3 2k+1 A2 + 3 3kA3.

Subtracting I, dividing by 3k+1 , and reducing modulo 3 again gives A ≡ 0 ( mod 3) , the same contradiction. □

12.3. Finite cohomological dimension of the IA subgroups. We use the following standard result of Charney–Vogtmann: Out (AΓ) is virtually torsion-free and has finite virtual cohomological dimension; their level-congruence subgroup is torsion-free [ 13 , Theorem 1.1 and §3]. Since that congruence subgroup contains IOut (AΓ), the latter has finite cohomological dimension. The inner group Inn( AΓ) = AΓ/Z (AΓ) is a finite-dimensional RAAG, and 

1 −→ Inn( AΓ) −→ IAut( AΓ) −→ IOut( AΓ) −→ 1

therefore gives cd IAut (AΓ) < ∞ by the Lyndon–Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence [ 10 ,Ch. VII]. IA rigidity gives cd IAut( HΓ) < ∞ as well. The central exact sequence 

1 −→ Zdeck Γ −→ IOut( HΓ) −→ IOut( AΓ) −→ 1, Zdeck Γ ∈ { 1, Z},

gives finite cohomological dimension for IOut (HΓ) as well. Notice that all these IA subgroups are themselves torsion-free by Proposition 8.6. 12.4. The full automorphism groups. Theorem 12.7. If Γ is finite and biconnected with at least three vertices, then both Aut (HΓ)

and Out( HΓ) are virtually torsion-free and have finite virtual cohomological dimension. Proof. Virtual torsion-freeness is Corollary 12.3. Choose a torsion-free finite-index subgroup 

Λ0 ≤ ΛΓ and take its inverse images in the two structural sequences 

1 → IAut( HΓ) → Aut( HΓ) → ΛΓ → 1

and 

1 → IOut( HΓ) → Out( HΓ) → ΛΓ → 1.

Each inverse image is torsion-free: a finite-order element has trivial image in the torsion-free quotient and then lies in the torsion-free kernel. The kernel terms have finite cohomological dimension by the preceding subsection, and cd (Λ 0) = dΓ by Theorem 12.5. The Lyndon– Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence [ 10 , Ch. VII] gives cd (G0) ≤ cd (N ) + dΓ < ∞ for both inverse images. □

13. Higher-rank lattice rigidity 

We use the cohomological quotient and the IA kernel from the biconnected structure theorem. The Wedderburn factors of the cubic algebra control the cohomological image, while residual torsion-free nilpotence forces the IA image of a suitable finite-index subgroup to be trivial. This gives a finite-image theorem whose rank bound is the largest matrix size occurring in the split Wedderburn quotient. Complete graphs show that this bound is optimal in general. Throughout the section, Γ is finite and biconnected with at least three vertices, and 

CΓ/ Jac (CΓ) ∼= ∏tr=1 Mmr (Q) is the decomposition from Theorem 4.12. Let G be a real semisimple Lie group with finite center and no compact factors, and let ∆ < G be an irreducible lattice. We always assume rank R G ≥ 2.13.1. Lattice and IA results. We use two distinct standard results about higher-rank irreducible lattices. Every finite-index subgroup ∆0 ≤ ∆ is again an irreducible lattice in G.The standard finite-abelianization corollary of the Margulis normal subgroup theorem [ 34 ,Theorem IV.4.10] therefore gives 

Hom(∆ 0, Z) = 0 . (13.1) This condition will be applied to the residually torsion-free nilpotent IA kernel. Separately, Wade proves that, if rank R G ≥ k, then every homomorphism 

∆0 −→ SL k(Z) (13.2) 78 J. LEI 

has finite image [ 49 , Proposition 7.3]. Since a finite-index subgroup of ∆ is again an irreducible lattice in the same ambient group, both results may be applied after passing to finite index, but their sources and roles are independent. For the IA kernel we use Proposition 8.6: both IAut (HΓ) and IOut (HΓ) are residually torsion-free nilpotent. The next lemma combines this property with (13.1). 

Lemma 13.1. Let L be finitely generated and suppose that Hom (L, Z) = 0 . If R is residually torsion-free nilpotent, then every homomorphism L → R is trivial. Proof. Suppose that f : L → R is nontrivial. Choose x ∈ L with f (x)̸ = 1 . Residual torsion-free nilpotence gives a homomorphism q : R → N to a torsion-free nilpotent group such that qf (x)̸ = 1 . The image N0 = qf (L) is therefore a nontrivial finitely generated torsion-free nilpotent group. Its abelianization is infinite: a finitely generated nilpotent group with finite abelianization is finite [ 39 , Ch. II], whereas N0 is nontrivial and torsion-free. The free abelian part of N ab 0 therefore maps onto Z. Composing L ↠ N0 with such a map contradicts 

Hom( L, Z) = 0 . □

A preliminary finite-image bound follows by using the full cohomological representation, whose rank is rank H1(HΓ; Z) = |V (Γ) | − 1. If rank R G ≥ | V (Γ) | − 1, then a homomorphism from ∆ to Aut (HΓ) or Out (HΓ) has finite image on first cohomology by (13.2) , after first killing the determinant. On a further finite-index subgroup its image lies in the corresponding IA subgroup and is trivial by Lemma 13.1. The rest of the section replaces this coarse bound by a generally smaller invariant of the cubic algebra. 13.2. The cubic rank bound. Define

An order in a split rational product need not decompose as a product of standard matrix orders. The invariant lattices permit the use of each rational coordinate without assuming such an integral decomposition. 

Proposition 13.3. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. Let G be a real semisimple Lie group with finite center and no compact factors, and let ∆ < G be an irreducible lattice. If rank R G ≥ Rcub (Γ) , then every homomorphism ∆ → ΛΓ has finite image. Proof. Let f : ∆ → ΛΓ. By (13.4) , the subgroup ∆0 = f −1(EΓ) has finite index in ∆. For each r with mr ≥ 2, compose qss f |∆0 with the coordinate homomorphism of Lemma 13.2. Since mr ≤ Rcub (Γ) , Wade’s lattice-rigidity result [ 49 , Proposition 7.3] makes every coordinate image finite. Intersecting their finitely many kernels produces a finite-index subgroup ∆1 ≤ ∆0

for which qss f (∆ 1) = 1 . The exact sequence (13.6) now gives f (∆ 1) ≤ EU . Equation (13.1) and Lemma 13.1—or the same nilpotent argument directly—give f (∆ 1) = 1 . Thus f (∆) is finite. □

The proof does not require Π to be a direct product. Making every coordinate projection trivial makes the image in the ambient rational product, and hence the semisimple image, trivial. The same argument treats mr = 2 ; the maximum with 2 in (13.3) ensures that the source lattice has real rank at least two. 13.3. Finite-image rigidity. Theorem 13.4. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. Let G be a real semisimple Lie group with finite center and no compact factors, and let ∆ < G be an irreducible lattice. Suppose that rank R G ≥ Rcub (Γ) . Then every homomorphism from ∆ to either Aut( HΓ) or Out( HΓ) has finite image. Proof. Let G be either target and let ρ : ∆ → G . The cohomological images of Aut (HΓ)

and Out (HΓ) are both ΛΓ, because inner automorphisms act trivially on abelianization. Proposition 13.3 therefore shows that the composite 

∆ ρ

−→ G −→ ΛΓ

has finite image. Its kernel ∆0 has finite index in ∆. The group ρ(∆ 0) lies in IAut (HΓ) in the automorphism case and in IOut (HΓ) in the outer case. Both groups are residually torsion-free nilpotent by Proposition 8.6. Equation (13.1) and Lemma 13.1 imply ρ(∆ 0) = 1 . Thus the original image is finite. □

The outer argument uses residual torsion-free nilpotence of IOut (HΓ) and does not assume that the central extension in Theorem 7.11 splits. 

Corollary 13.5. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. If d ≥

Rcub (Γ) + 1 , then every homomorphism from SL d(Z) to either Aut (HΓ) or Out (HΓ) has finite image. 80 J. LEI 

Proof. Apply Theorem 13.4 to the irreducible lattice SL d(Z) < SL d(R) and use rank R SL d(R) = 

d − 1. □

13.4. Sharpness. Proposition 13.6. The matrix-size inequality in Theorem 13.4 cannot be lowered by one uniformly over all biconnected defining graphs. Proof. Let Γ = Kn with n ≥ 4. Then 

HKn ∼= Zn−1, Aut( HKn ) = Out( HKn ) ∼= GL n−1(Z).

Because HKn is abelian, its quadratic and cubic relation spaces are the entire degree-two and degree-three homogeneous components of the free Lie algebra, and its separator arrangement is empty. The combined stabilizer is therefore the full GL n−1. Moreover, CKn = Mn−1(Q)

and Rcub (Kn) = n − 1. On the other hand, the standard inclusion SL n−1(Z) ↪→ GL n−1(Z) = Out (HKn ) has infinite image. Its ambient real group has rank n − 2, exactly one less than 

Rcub (Kn). Thus the sufficient condition rank R G ≥ mr cannot in general be replaced by 

rank R G ≥ mr − 1. □

14. Aut–Out finiteness comparisons 

This section proves the unconditional Aut–Out equivalence in type F2, a conditional higher-degree analogue, an all-degree biconnected version, and the SIL-free sufficient condition for finite presentation. In this section, “type F2” and “finitely presented” are synonymous. They must not be confused with type F P 2 or with the condition of containing a rank-two free group. 14.1. Finite presentation: automorphisms versus outer automorphisms. We use the following relative form of the standard presentation theorem for extensions. 

Lemma 14.1. Let N , P , and Q be groups fitting into a short exact sequence 

1 −→ N −→ P π

−−→ Q −→ 1.

Let Q0 ≤ Q be a subgroup and put P0 = π−1(Q0) ≤ P . Assume that N is finitely generated, P0

is finitely presented, and Q admits a finite presentation relative to the coefficient subgroup Q0.Then P is finitely presented. Proof. Choose a finite relative presentation 

Q = ⟨Q0, x 1, . . . , x m | r1, . . . , r k⟩

and lifts ˜xi ∈ P . Let Y be a finite generating set of N . For every i and y ∈ Y , choose Y -words representing the two conjugates ˜x±1 

> i

y˜x∓1 

> i

. Replace the finitely many Q0-coefficients in each rj

by elements of P0 and choose a Y -word representing the resulting lifted relator. Start with P0 ∗ F (x1, . . . , x m) and impose these finitely many conjugation and lifted-relator relations. The resulting group maps onto P ; call it ̂ P . The natural map P0 →̂ P is injective, because its composite with ̂ P → P is the original inclusion P0 ↪→ P . In particular, we may regard N as a subgroup of ̂ P . The conjugation relations make this copy of N normal. After quotienting by N , the coefficient group becomes P0/N ∼= Q0 and the displayed relative presentation is exactly the chosen presentation of Q. Hence ̂ P /N → Q is an isomorphism, so the kernel of ̂ P → P lies in N . The restriction of that map to N is the original inclusion 

N ↪ → P and is injective. Thus the kernel is trivial. A finite presentation of P0 therefore turns this relative presentation into an ordinary finite presentation of P . □AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 81 

If Q is finitely presented and Q0 is finitely generated, then Q is finitely presented relative to 

Q0: adjoin a finite generating set of Q0 to an ordinary finite presentation and equate those generators to chosen words in the original generators. Now let Γ be finite and connected. Choose a vertex t and let τ = ct|HΓ . Conjugation gives a homomorphism κ : AΓ −→ Aut (HΓ). Proposition 7.8 gives ker κ = CAΓ (HΓ) = Z(AΓ). If U is the set of universal vertices, then AΓ = AU × AΓ−U and hence 

κ(AΓ) ∼= AΓ/Z (AΓ) ∼= AΓ−U . (14.1) Thus κ(AΓ) is a finitely presented RAAG. Let C = ⟨[τ ]⟩ ≤ Out (HΓ). Since AΓ = HΓ⟨t⟩, the full preimage of C under Aut (HΓ) →

Out( HΓ) is exactly

Theorem 14.2. For every finite connected graph Γ,

Aut( HΓ) is finitely presented ⇐⇒ Out( HΓ) is finitely presented .

Proof. Because Γ is connected, HΓ and its quotient Inn (HΓ) are finitely generated. If Aut (HΓ)

is finitely presented, quotienting by a finite generating set of Inn (HΓ) adds finitely many normal relators and gives a finite presentation of Out( HΓ).Conversely, suppose that Out( HΓ) is finitely presented. Apply Lemma 14.1 to 

1 −→ Inn( HΓ) −→ Aut( HΓ) −→ Out( HΓ) −→ 1

with Q0 = C. The kernel is finitely generated, C is finitely generated, and its full preimage is the finitely presented RAAG in (14.1)–(14.2). Hence Aut( HΓ) is finitely presented. □

The argument does not require HΓ to be finitely presented: it uses the finitely presented deck-conjugation preimage rather than finite presentability of Inn( HΓ).14.2. Higher finiteness properties. The unconditional degree-two theorem is stronger than the general extension result below, because it requires no finiteness assumption on HΓ. In higher degrees one obtains an equivalence whenever HΓ itself is of type Fn. By the Bestvina–Brady finiteness theorem, this hypothesis is equivalent to (n − 1) -connectivity of the flag complex ∆Γ

[3]. We isolate the extension rules used in this subsection. 

Proposition 14.3 (Permanence rules for type Fn). Adopt the standard convention that every group is of type F0, and let n ≥ 1.

(i) If 1 → N → G → Q → 1 is exact and N, Q are of type Fn, then G is of type Fn.

(ii) If 1 → N → G → Q → 1 is exact, G is of type Fn, and N is of type Fn−1, then Q is of type Fn.

(iii) In such an extension, if N is of type F∞, then G ∈ Fn if and only if Q ∈ Fn.

(iv) Type Fn is invariant under passage between finite-index subgroups and finite-index supergroups. 

(v) Type Fn passes to retracts. 

(vi) If G = G1 ∗A G2, the groups G1, G 2 are of type Fn, and A is of type Fn−1, then G is of type Fn.Proof. Statements (i) and (ii) are the standard extension and quotient criteria for homotopical finiteness properties; see [ 21 , Ch. 7]. For n = 1 , statement (ii) is simply the fact that a 82 J. LEI 

quotient of a finitely generated group is finitely generated. Statement (iii) follows by applying (i) and (ii), since an F∞ group is of type Fn and Fn−1.For (iv), a finite-index subgroup corresponds to a finite-sheeted cover of a classifying space and therefore inherits a finite n-skeleton. Conversely, if H ≤ G has finite index and is of type 

Fn, its normal core K is a finite-index subgroup of H and hence is of type Fn by the first direction. The extension 1 → K → G → G/K → 1 has finite quotient, which is of type F∞,so (i) gives G ∈ Fn.Statement (v) is the standard domination criterion for classifying spaces: a group retract gives a homotopy domination of a classifying space for the retract by one for the ambient group, and a finitely dominated n-skeleton can be replaced by a finite one; see again [ 21 ,Ch. 7]. For (vi), choose classifying spaces for G1, G 2 with finite n-skeleta and one for A with finite (n − 1) -skeleton, and realize the two inclusions by cellular maps. The usual Bass–Serre graph-of-spaces construction attaches the mapping cylinder of the edge space to the two vertex spaces. Its universal cover is a tree of contractible spaces and is therefore contractible, while its n-skeleton is finite because the edge-space cells acquire one extra dimension. It is therefore a classifying space for G1 ∗A G2 with finite n-skeleton. □

Lemma 14.4. Let Γ be a finite connected graph with at least two vertices and let U be its set of universal vertices. Then 

Z(HΓ) = HΓ ∩ Z(AΓ) = ker (ZU Σ

−−→ Z),

a free abelian group of rank max (|U | − 1, 0) . In particular Z(HΓ) is of type F∞, and HΓ ∈ Fk

if and only if Inn( HΓ) ∈ Fk, for every k.Proof. By Proposition 7.8, CAΓ (HΓ) = Z(AΓ), so Z(HΓ) = HΓ ∩ CAΓ (HΓ) = HΓ ∩ Z(AΓ).The center Z(AΓ) = AU is free abelian on the universal vertices [ 43 ], and χΓ restricts on it to the augmentation ZU → Z; hence HΓ ∩ Z(AΓ) is the augmentation kernel, free abelian of rank max (|U | − 1, 0) and of type F∞. Applying Proposition 14.3 to 1 → Z(HΓ) → HΓ →

Inn( HΓ) → 1, whose kernel is of type F∞, gives HΓ ∈ Fk ⇔ Inn( HΓ) ∈ Fk. □

Theorem 14.5. Let Γ be a finite connected graph and let n ≥ 1. If HΓ is of type Fn, then 

Aut( HΓ) ∈ Fn ⇐⇒ Out( HΓ) ∈ Fn.

Proof. If Γ has a single vertex then HΓ = 1 and the statement is trivial, so assume Γ has at least two vertices. By Lemma 14.4, HΓ ∈ Fn gives Inn (HΓ) ∈ Fn, hence also Inn (HΓ) ∈ Fn−1.Apply Proposition 14.3 to 1 → Inn (HΓ) → Aut (HΓ) → Out (HΓ) → 1. If Out (HΓ) ∈ Fn,then Aut (HΓ) ∈ Fn, both kernel and quotient being of type Fn. If Aut (HΓ) ∈ Fn, then 

Out( HΓ) ∈ Fn, because the kernel Inn( HΓ) is of type Fn−1. □

For n = 2 , this gives the special case of Theorem 14.2 in which HΓ is finitely presented; that theorem removes the hypothesis on HΓ entirely. For biconnected graphs the finiteness hypothesis can be dropped in every degree. 

Theorem 14.6. Let Γ be a finite biconnected graph with at least three vertices. Then Aut (HΓ) ∈

Fn ⇔ Out( HΓ) ∈ Fn for every n ≥ 1, with no finiteness hypothesis on HΓ.Proof. If Γ has a universal vertex u, then ∆Γ is a cone with apex u, hence contractible, so HΓ

is of type F∞ [3] and the claim follows from Theorem 14.5. Otherwise Γ has no universal vertex, so Z(AΓ) = 1 and, by (7.26) , the central sequence of Theorem 7.11 has infinite cyclic kernel generated by the deck class. Put C = ⟨[τ ]⟩ ∼= Z; then

AΓ of (14.1) –(14.2) , of type F∞. Let π : Aut (HΓ) → Out (HΓ) be the quotient map, and let

kernel of the action on H1—and C ≤ Z( IOut (HΓ)) , every conjugate qCq −1 lies in Z( IOut (HΓ)) ;these conjugates commute, so D is abelian. for every n. □

The examples of Section 15 show that neither the hypothesis HΓ ∈ Fn in Theorem 14.5 nor the biconnectedness hypothesis in Theorem 14.6 can be omitted: a graph with a cut vertex can have Out( HΓ) of type F∞ while Aut( HΓ) is of type F3 but not F4.14.3. A graph-theoretic sufficient condition for finite presentation. The absence of a SIL gives a direct graph-theoretic sufficient condition. 

Theorem 14.7. If Γ is finite and biconnected with at least three vertices and has no SIL, then both Aut( HΓ) and Out( HΓ) are finitely presented. Proof. Proposition 11.7 says that IOut (AΓ) is finitely generated abelian. The central exact sequence (7.25) then makes IOut (HΓ) a finitely generated nilpotent group of class at most two. It is therefore polycyclic and finitely presented [40, Chapter 1]. The cohomological image ΛΓ is also finitely presented. Indeed, it has a finite-index subgroup 

EΓ, and Propositions 6.16 and 6.11 express EΓ as an extension of a finitely generated nilpotent group by a group commensurable with a semisimple arithmetic group. The first group is polycyclic, and the second is finitely presented by Borel–Serre [ 6 ]; hence EΓ, and therefore ΛΓ,is finitely presented. The structural sequence 

1 −→ IOut( HΓ) −→ Out( HΓ) −→ ΛΓ −→ 1

has finitely presented kernel and quotient. The standard presentation construction for an exten-sion therefore shows that Out (HΓ) is finitely presented [ 10 , Chapter VII]. The automorphism conclusion follows from Theorem 14.2. □

The theorem applies even when HΓ is not finitely presented. For example, HCn is not finitely presented for n ≥ 5, while Out( HCn ) is virtually infinite cyclic. 15. Counterexamples, universality, and nonlinearity 

The preceding structure theorems do not impose uniform restrictions on finite presentability or linearity. The examples below establish four distinct conclusions. First, a Bestvina–Brady group of type F∞ can have automorphism and outer automorphism groups that are finitely generated but not finitely presented. Second, the unconditional Aut–Out equivalence in type 

F2 can fail in higher degrees for connected graphs with cut vertices. Third, the automorphism groups of arbitrary right-angled Artin groups occur inside the automorphism groups of type-F

Bestvina–Brady groups with biconnected defining graphs. Fourth, these ambient groups can be nonlinear even though their cohomological images are arithmetic up to finite index. 84 J. LEI 

15.1. A type-F∞ counterexample. Let Kn denote the edgeless graph on n vertices. Let 

P4 = a − b − c − d be the four-vertex path and put 

Γ∗ = K3 ∗ P4, B = HΓ∗ . (15.1) 

Theorem 15.1. The graph Γ∗ is biconnected and B is of type F∞, but neither Aut (B) nor 

Out( B) is finitely presented. Both groups are finitely generated. 

Write 

G1 = AK3 = F3, G2 = AP4 , AΓ∗ = G1 × G2, (15.2) and let χi : Gi → Z be the all-ones characters. Thus 

B = {(g1, g 2) ∈ G1 × G2 : χ1(g1) + χ2(g2) = 0 } . (15.3) The proof of Theorem 15.1 occupies the rest of this subsection. 

The two factor kernels are intrinsic. We begin with the centralizer property that detects the two factors. 

Lemma 15.2. For i = 1 , 2, every nontrivial normal subgroup N ⊴ Gi has trivial centralizer in 

Gi.Proof. For G1 = F3 this is the classical centralizer property of a nonabelian free group [ 32 ,Ch. I]. For G2, the graph P4 is not a nontrivial join. Hence AP4 is acylindrically hyperbolic [15 , Theorem 4.4]. It is also torsion-free. Let N ⊴ AP4 be nontrivial. Since AP4 is torsion-free, 

N is infinite, and normality makes N an s-normal subgroup. It therefore acts non-elementarily in the same acylindrical action [ 36 , Lemma 7.1]. By Osin’s theorem [ 36 , Theorem 1.1], N

contains independent loxodromic elements g and h. Now 

CAP4 (N ) ⊆ CAP4 (g) ∩ CAP4 (h).

Each centralizer on the right is virtually cyclic [ 36 , Corollary 6.9]. If their intersection were infinite, an infinite-order element in it would be commensurable with both g and h inside the two virtually cyclic centralizers. The two loxodromics would then have the same pair of limit points, contradicting their independence. Thus the displayed intersection is finite, and torsion-freeness makes it trivial. □

Set

so x−1Ψ( x) ∈ CAΓ∗ (B) = 1 by (15.9) . The two compositions are therefore the identity, and the factor homomorphisms are automorphisms.
