For injectivity, suppose that Ψ fixes B pointwise. For x ∈ AΓ∗ and b ∈ B, normality of B

gives 

Ψ( x)bΨ( x)−1 = Ψ( xbx −1) = xbx −1.

Hence x−1Ψ( x) ∈ CAΓ∗ (B). The two projections of B are surjective, so (15.9) gives Ψ( x) = x

for every x, proving injectivity. □

The ambient automorphism group also separates factorwise. 

Lemma 15.4. There is a natural isomorphism 

Aut( AΓ∗ ) ∼= Aut( F3) × Aut( AP4 ).86 J. LEI 

Proof. Use the Laurence–Servatius generating theorem [ 28 , 43 ]. The complement graph of Γ∗

has the two nonisomorphic connected components K3 and P4, so graph symmetries cannot exchange the factors. Inversions are factorwise, and the join makes every partial conjugation factorwise as well. It remains to exclude cross-factor transvections. If a vertex of K3 were transvected by a vertex of P4, the domination condition would require that vertex of P4 to be universal in P4.No such vertex exists. In the other direction, the star of a K3-vertex omits the other two K3-vertices and therefore cannot contain the link of a P4-vertex. Thus every Laurence–Servatius generator preserves both factors. The reverse inclusion is evident. □

For i = 1 , 2, let 

Si = {α ∈ Aut( Gi) : χiα = χi}. (15.12) By Proposition 15.3 and Lemma 15.4, 

P = S1 × S2 (15.13) has index two in Aut (B). Indeed, an ambient pair preserving {± χ} must act on the two factor characters by the same sign, and simultaneous inversion of all standard generators realizes the negative sign. 

The two height stabilizers. The first factor supplies the obstruction to finite presentation. 

Lemma 15.5. The group S1 is not finitely presented. Proof. Let ρab : Aut (F3) → GL 3(Z) be the action on abelianization. Choose a basis x1, x 2, x 3

and let χ0(x1) = 1 , χ0(x2) = χ0(x3) = 0 . Since ρab is surjective by Nielsen transformations and GL 3(Z) is transitive on primitive covectors [ 32 , Ch. I], there is θ ∈ Aut (F3) such that 

χ0 = χ1θ−1. Therefore 

θS 1θ−1 = {α ∈ Aut( F3) : χ0α = χ0}. (15.14) Let Row 3,1 ≤ GL 3(Z) be the subgroup of matrices whose first row is the first row of the identity, and put 

IAR 3,1 = ( ρab )−1(Row 3,1).

With the convention that the columns of the abelianization matrix record the images of the basis elements, the equality χ0α = χ0 says exactly that the first row of ρab (α) is (1 , 0, 0) . Hence the group on the right of (15.14) is IAR 3,1.Put 

P0 = ker χ0 = ⟨⟨ x2, x 3⟩⟩ F3 .

After permuting the basis in the notation of Krstić–McCool, their positive stabilizer T (F3) is precisely IAR 3,1: it preserves P0 and induces the identity, rather than inversion, on the quotient 

F3/P 0 ∼= Z generated by the image of x1. They show that T (F3) has index two in the setwise stabilizer S(F3) = Stab (P0), and Corollary 3 states that S(F3) is not finitely presented [ 27 ,pp. 598–600, Corollary 3]. Finite presentability is invariant under passage between finite-index subgroups and overgroups, so T (F3) ∼= IAR 3,1 is not finitely presented. Thus S1 is not finitely presented. □

The second factor is different: only its finite generation is needed. 

Lemma 15.6. The group S2 is finitely generated. Proof. Consider the cohomological representation ρ : Aut (AP4 ) −→ GL 4(Z). Its kernel is 

IAut (AP4 ), which is finitely generated by Day’s theorem [ 16 , Theorem B], and this kernel is contained in S2.AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 87 

The Laurence–Servatius generators [ 28 , 43 ] show that ρ(Aut (AP4 )) is generated by the four vertex inversions, the reflection of the path, and the four domination transvections 

a 7 → ab, a 7 → ac, d 7 → db, d 7 → dc. 

Their cohomological images commute and generate a free abelian group of rank four. The inversions and the reflection form a finite group that normalizes this lattice. Hence ρ( Aut (AP4 )) 

is virtually Z4. If Q is any subgroup, then Q ∩ Z4 is a finitely generated abelian group of finite index in Q; thus every subgroup is finitely generated. In particular, ρ(S2) is finitely generated. The exact sequence 

1 −→ IAut( AP4 ) −→ S2 −→ ρ(S2) −→ 1

then proves the claim. □

Proof of Theorem 15.1. Deleting any vertex from K3 ∗ P4 leaves a join of two nonempty graphs, and hence a connected graph. Thus Γ∗ is biconnected. Its flag complex is the simplicial join of the flag complexes of K3 and P4. The latter is a contractible tree, so the join is contractible. Bestvina–Brady Morse theory therefore implies that B is of type F∞ [3]. Suppose that the index-two subgroup P = S1 × S2 from (15.13) were finitely presented. Choose a finite generating set of S2, which exists by Lemma 15.6, and add those generators as relators to a finite presentation of P . The resulting quotient is S1, contradicting Lemma 15.5. Hence P , and therefore its finite-index overgroup Aut (B), is not finitely presented, since finite presentability is invariant under passage between finite-index subgroups and supergroups by Proposition 14.3(iv). The Aut–Out equivalence of Theorem 14.2 now shows that Out (B) is not finitely presented either. Finally, both groups are finitely generated by Theorem 10.8. □

The proof above is deliberately specific to K3 ∗ P4. In particular, the double-centralizer recognition and the finite-generation computation for S2 are not being asserted for arbitrary joins Kr ∗ Ps.15.2. Failure of higher-degree Aut–Out equivalence. The unconditional degree-two equivalence of Theorem 14.2 does not extend to higher degrees without the additional hypothesis 

HΓ ∈ Fn. We exhibit a finite connected graph for which Out (HΓ) is of type F∞ while Aut (HΓ)

is of type F3 but not F4. We first determine the outer automorphism group of a two-factor free product. 

Lemma 15.7. Let a group G act without inversions on a tree T with trivial edge stabilizers. If v̸ = w have nontrivial stabilizers, put A = Gv, B = Gw, and H = ⟨A, B ⟩. Then the natural map A ∗ B → H is an isomorphism, and the quotient of the minimal H-invariant subtree by 

H is the segment [v, w ], with the two endpoint groups A, B and trivial groups on its interior vertices and edges. In particular, if H = G and T /G consists of one edge, then dT (v, w ) = 1 .Proof. Let I = [ v, w ]. A nontrivial element of A cannot fix the first edge of I, and a nontrivial element of B cannot fix the last edge, because edge stabilizers are trivial. The usual half-tree ping-pong argument therefore shows that every nonempty reduced word alternating between 

A \ { 1} and B \ { 1} moves an interior point of I; hence A ∗ B → H is injective. It is surjective by definition. The translates of I by reduced words attach successively at the endpoint fixed by the last syllable. Triviality of edge stabilizers prevents a fold at an attaching edge. Their union TH is therefore an H-tree whose quotient is exactly the graph-of-groups segment I, with only the two endpoint vertex groups nontrivial. Every nonempty H-invariant subtree contains v: it is 

A-invariant, and the nearest-point projection of the unique A-fixed vertex v to that subtree is 88 J. LEI 

again fixed by A; the same argument puts w in the subtree. It therefore contains every H-translate of I. Thus TH is the minimal H-invariant subtree. This is also the standard subgroup normal-form argument for a tree action [ 42 , Section I.4]. If H = G, its minimal subtree is T ;comparing the quotient segment with the one-edge quotient T /G gives dT (v, w ) = 1 . □

Lemma 15.8. Let K and L be noncyclic, freely indecomposable groups that are not isomorphic. Then Out( K ∗ L) ∼= Aut( K) × Aut( L).Proof. Write G = K ∗L and let T be its Bass–Serre tree, with K and L the stabilizers of the two ends of an edge. By the uniqueness part of Grushko’s theorem [ 23 , 44 ], every Φ ∈ Aut (G) carries 

K and L to conjugates; composing with an inner automorphism we may assume Φ( K) = K

and Φ( L) = wLw −1. The stabilizers K and wLw −1 generate G, so Lemma 15.7 shows that their fixed vertices are adjacent in T . The L-type vertices adjacent to the vertex fixed by K

are exactly the vertices fixed by kLk −1 with k ∈ K; hence wLw −1 = kLk −1 for some k ∈ K.After composing with ck−1 , the representative preserves both K and L. Thus every outer class is represented by a factorwise automorphism, giving a surjection Aut (K) × Aut (L) → Out (G).It is injective: if a factorwise automorphism is inner, say cg, then g normalizes both K and L;free factors are self-normalizing in a free product by the normal-form theorem [ 32 , Ch. IV], so 

g ∈ K ∩ L = 1 and the factorwise automorphism is the identity. □

The inner directions of the two factors are not lost: the partial conjugation of K by an element l ∈ L represents a pair (id , ι ) with ι ∈ Inn (L), so Inn (K) × Inn (L) survives inside 

Aut( K) × Aut( L). This is why the factors contribute their full automorphism groups. The obstruction to higher finiteness is carried by a holomorph. 

Proposition 15.9. For m ≥ 5 let K = HCm and Hol (K) = K ⋊ Aut (K). Then Hol (K) is of type F3 but not of type F4.Proof. As Cm has no universal vertex, Lemma 14.4 gives Z(K) = 1 , so Inn( K) ∼= K and 

Hol( K) −→ Aut( K) ×Out( K) Aut( K), (k, α ) 7 −→ (ckα, α ),

is an isomorphism onto the fiber product. The deck subgroup D = IOut (HCm ) ∼= Z has finite index in Out (K) by Theorem 8.12, and its full preimage in Aut (K) is P := κ(ACm ) ∼= ACm

by (14.1) – (14.2) . Hence P ×D P has finite index in Hol (K). Identifying P ∼= ACm so that 

P → D ∼= Z becomes the all-ones character χ : ACm → Z with kernel K,

P ×D P = {(a, b ) ∈ ACm × ACm : χ(a) = χ(b)}.

Inverting every standard generator of the second factor carries χ−χ to χ+χ. Since ACm ×ACm ∼=

ACm∗Cm , the join, and χ + χ is the all-ones character of ACm∗Cm , P ×D P ∼= HCm∗Cm . The flag complex of a join is the join of flag complexes, so ∆Cm∗Cm = ∆ Cm ∗ ∆Cm ∼= S1 ∗ S1 ∼= S3,which is 2-connected but not 3-connected. By the Bestvina–Brady criterion [ 3 ], HCm∗Cm is of type F3 but not F4, and the same holds for its finite-index overgroup Hol (K), since type Fn is invariant under passage to finite-index subgroups and supergroups by Proposition 14.3(iv). □

Theorem 15.10. For m ≥ 5 let Γm = Cm ∨ K3 be the one-point union of an m-cycle and a triangle. Then Out (HΓm ) is of type F∞, while Aut (HΓm ) is of type F3 but not F4. In particular the degree-n Aut–Out equivalence fails for every n ≥ 4.Proof. The blocks of Γm are Cm and the triangle K3, so Theorem 9.3 gives HΓm ∼= K ∗ L

with K = HCm and L = HK3 ∼= Z2. These factors are noncyclic, freely indecomposable, and non-isomorphic, one being nonabelian and the other abelian. By Lemma 15.8, Out (HΓm ) ∼= Aut (HCm ) × GL 2(Z). Both factors are of type F∞: Aut (HCm )

contains the right-angled Artin group κ(ACm ) ∼= ACm with finite index; right-angled Artin AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 89 

groups have finite Salvetti classifying spaces [ 13 ], and type F∞ is invariant under passage between finite-index subgroups and supergroups by Proposition 14.3(iv). Moreover, GL 2(Z) is virtually free [42, Ch. I]. Hence Out( HΓm ) is of type F∞.For Aut (HΓm ), put A = Aut (K) × Aut (L). Since Z(K ∗ L) = 1 , the inner automorphism group is naturally K∗L, and the factorwise section in Lemma 15.8 gives Aut (K∗L) ∼= (K∗L)⋊A.The universal property of free products with amalgamation gives the elementary identity 

(K ∗ L) ⋊ A ∼= ( K ⋊ A) ∗A (L ⋊ A). (15.15) Indeed, both sides are generated by copies of K, L, and A, with the same relations inside these three groups and the same conjugation relations describing the action of A; the two universal properties give homomorphisms inverse to one another. Since A acts on K through its Aut (K)

factor and on L through its Aut( L) factor, 

K ⋊ A ∼= Hol( K) × Aut( L), L ⋊ A ∼= Aut( K) × Hol( L).

Thus (15.15) becomes 

Aut( K ∗ L) ∼= ( Hol( K) × Aut( L)) ∗Aut( K)×Aut( L) 

> (

Aut( K) × Hol( L)).

Here Hol (L) = Z2 ⋊ GL 2(Z) and Aut (L) = GL 2(Z) are of type F∞, while Hol (K) is of type 

F3 \ F4 by Proposition 15.9. The two vertex groups are thus of type F3 and the edge group of type F∞, so the amalgam is of type F3 by Proposition 14.3(vi). On the other hand the retraction K ∗ L → K killing L, together with the projection Aut (K) × Aut (L) → Aut (K),induces a retraction Aut (HΓm ) → Hol (K). Type F4 passes to retracts by Proposition 14.3(v), and Hol( K) is not of type F4; therefore neither is Aut( HΓm ). □

Thus the deck direction and the inner direction combine, through the fiber product of Proposition 15.9, into an obstruction that lives in Aut (HΓm ) but disappears in Out (HΓm ),where the inner factor becomes trivial. Theorem 14.5 forbids such a gap when HΓ is of type 

Fn, equivalently when the flag complex ∆Γ is (n − 1) -connected. Here HΓm = HCm ∗ Z2 is only finitely generated and not finitely presented, since ∆Γm ≃ S1 is not simply connected; thus the additional hypothesis already fails for n = 2 .15.3. Join universality. The preceding counterexample is graph-specific, but a different join construction gives a uniform embedding theorem. Its full Aut and Out embeddings are elementary consequences of a direct-product description; the IA statement additionally uses the biconnected rigidity theorem. 

Theorem 15.11. Let ∆ be a nonempty finite graph and let Γ = ∆ ∗ K2. Then Γ is biconnected and 

HΓ ∼= A∆ × Z. (15.16) 

In particular, HΓ is a right-angled Artin group of type F . There are injective homomorphisms 

Aut( A∆) ↪→ Aut( HΓ), Out( A∆) ↪→ Out( HΓ). (15.17) 

Moreover, 

IAut( HΓ) ∼= IAut( A∆). (15.18) 

Proof. Let p, q be the vertices of K2. Since the join becomes a direct product, 

AΓ = A∆ × ⟨ p, q ⟩ ∼= A∆ × Z2.

Writing the two central coordinates additively gives 

HΓ = {(g, m, n ) : χ∆(g) + m + n = 0 }.

The map 

A∆ × Z −→ HΓ, (g, m ) 7 −→ (g, m, −χ∆(g) − m) (15.19) 90 J. LEI 

is an isomorphism, with inverse given by projection onto the first two coordinates. This proves (15.16) . It also identifies HΓ with the RAAG obtained from ∆ by adding one universal vertex, so its Salvetti complex is finite [13]. The graph Γ is biconnected. Deleting p leaves q adjacent to every remaining vertex, and deleting q is symmetric. Deleting a vertex of ∆ leaves the edge {p, q }, both of whose endpoints are adjacent to all remaining vertices. The hypothesis that ∆ is nonempty ensures that Γ has at least three vertices. For an arbitrary group G, extension by the identity defines 

j : Aut( G) −→ Aut( G × Z), j(α)( g, n ) = ( α(g), n ).

Restriction to G × { 0} proves that j is injective. It sends inner automorphisms to inner automorphisms and therefore descends to a map on outer automorphism groups. If j(α) is conjugation by (g, n ), then its restriction to G × { 0} is conjugation by g. Thus α is inner, and the induced outer map is injective. Apply this observation to G = A∆ and use (15.19) to obtain (15.17). It remains to prove the IA assertion. We use the following elementary universal-vertex observation. If U is the set of universal vertices of a finite graph Θ and Θ0 = Θ − U , then 

IAut( AΘ) ∼= IAut( AΘ0 ). (15.20) Indeed, 

AΘ = AΘ0 × ZU , Z(AΘ) = ZU ,

where AΘ0 has trivial center unless it is trivial. An IA automorphism preserves the center and fixes it pointwise, since the center injects into the abelianization. Modulo the center it induces an IA automorphism of AΘ0 . If an element of AΘ0 acquired a nonzero central coordinate, that coordinate would remain detected in the abelianization, contradicting the IA condition. Thus the automorphism is the product of an IA automorphism of AΘ0 with the identity on ZU ,proving (15.20). The non-universal core of Γ = ∆ ∗ K2 is the same as the non-universal core of ∆. Equation (15.20) therefore gives IAut (AΓ) ∼= IAut (A∆). Since Γ is biconnected, Theorem 7.10 gives 

IAut( HΓ) ∼= IAut( AΓ), and (15.18) follows. □

For general ∆ the inclusions in (15.17) are only inclusions: no finite-index, retraction, or isomorphism claim is made, and the full embeddings and the IA isomorphism have different proofs, the former coming directly from (15.19) and the latter using biconnected IA rigidity. When ∆ has no universal vertex—equivalently, when A∆ has trivial center [ 43 ]—both inclusions are retractions, and the full automorphism group of HΓ is computed exactly. This follows from an elementary description of the automorphisms of a centerless direct product with Z.

Lemma 15.12. Let G be a group with trivial center. For h ∈ Hom (G, Z), α ∈ Aut (G), and 

k ∈ {± 1}, define 

Φh,α,k : G × Z −→ G × Z,

(g, n ) 7 −→ (α(g), h (α(g)) + kn ). (15.21) 

Every automorphism of G × Z has a unique expression of this form. The assignment 

(h, α, k ) 7 −→ Φh,α,k 

defines an isomorphism 

Hom( G, Z) ⋊ (Aut( G) × {± 1}) ∼=

where (α, k ) acts on h by 

(α, k ) · h = k (h ◦ α−1).AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 91 

Inner automorphisms correspond to the triples (0 , c g, 1) , and hence 

Out( G × Z) ∼= Hom( G, Z) ⋊ (Out( G) × {± 1}).

Proof. The center Z(G × Z) = 1 × Z is characteristic, so an automorphism φ restricts to an automorphism of 1 × Z and φ(1 , 1) = (1 , k ) with k = ±1. Write φ(g, 0) = ( α(g), f (g)) ; both coordinates are homomorphisms in g, and 

φ(g, n ) = (α(g), f (g) + kn ).

The map α is surjective because the first coordinate of φ is, and injective because α(g) = 1 

forces φ(g, 0) ∈ 1 × Z = Z(G × Z), whence (g, 0) ∈ Z(G × Z) and g = 1 . Thus α ∈ Aut( G).Put h = f ◦α−1. Then the preceding formula is exactly (15.21) , and h is uniquely determined. Conversely, every triple (h, α, k ) in the statement defines an automorphism, with

= Φ −k(h◦α), α −1, k .

A direct calculation gives

Hom -factor descends from Aut (G) to Out (G) because h ◦ cg = h for every homomorphism h

into an abelian group. □

Theorem 15.13. Let ∆ be a nonempty finite graph with no universal vertex (so |V (∆) | ≥ 2), and let Γ = ∆ ∗ K2. Then 

Aut( HΓ) ∼= ZV (∆) ⋊ (Aut( A∆) × Z/2Z), Out( HΓ) ∼= ZV (∆) ⋊ (Out( A∆) × Z/2Z), (15.23) 

and the embeddings (15.17) are the inclusions of the Aut (A∆)- and Out (A∆)-factors of the semidirect complements. In particular both embeddings are retracts: there are surjective homomorphisms 

Aut( HΓ) −→ Aut( A∆), Out( HΓ) −→ Out( A∆)

restricting to the identity on the embedded copies. Every automorphism group of a centerless right-angled Artin group is therefore a retract of the automorphism group of a biconnected Bestvina–Brady group of type F .Proof. A single vertex is universal in its own graph, so the nonempty graph ∆ has |V (∆) | ≥ 2,and Z(A∆) = 1 by Servatius’s centralizer theorem [ 43 ]. Theorem 15.11 gives HΓ ∼= A∆ × Z

with Γ biconnected and HΓ of type F . Lemma 15.12 applies with G = A∆, and Hom (A∆, Z) ∼=

ZV (∆) . Under the resulting identifications, the embedding j from the proof of Theorem 15.11 is α 7 → (0 , α, 1) , and the projection (h, α, k ) 7 → α is a homomorphism by the displayed composition rule; it restricts to the identity on the embedded Aut (A∆). Both maps send inner automorphisms to inner automorphisms and induce the outer statements. □

Remark 15.14 (Finite presentation of the IA subgroup does not control the full group) . Take 

∆ = K3. Then HK3∗K2

∼= F3 × Z is a right-angled Artin group. Hence its full automorphism group is finitely presented by Day’s peak-reduction theorem [ 17 ], and its outer automorphism group is finitely presented by Theorem 14.2. On the other hand, Theorem 15.11 gives 

IAut (HK3∗K2 ) ∼= IAut (F3) = IA 3, which is not finitely presented by [ 27 , Theorem 1]. Thus non-finite-presentability of the IA subgroup does not imply non-finite-presentability of the full automorphism group. Alternatively, Theorem 15.13 identifies Aut (HK3∗K2 ) with Z3 ⋊

( Aut (F3) × Z/2Z), an extension of a finitely presented group by a finitely generated abelian group, from which finite presentability also follows. 92 J. LEI 

15.4. Nonlinear examples. The join construction also shows that arithmeticity of the cohomological image cannot be promoted to linearity of the full automorphism group. 

Corollary 15.15. For n ≥ 1, put Γn = Kn ∗ K2. Then Γn is biconnected and HΓn ∼= Fn × Z

is of type F . Moreover, 

Aut( HΓn ) is nonlinear for n ≥ 3,

and 

Out( HΓn ) is nonlinear for n ≥ 4.

Proof. The product description and the embeddings 

Aut( Fn) ↪→ Aut( Fn × Z), Out( Fn) ↪→ Out( Fn × Z)

are the specialization of Theorem 15.11 to ∆ = Kn. Formanek–Procesi proved that Aut (Fn)

is nonlinear for n ≥ 3 [20 ]. Since every subgroup of a linear group is linear, the assertion for 

Aut( HΓn ) follows. For the outer assertion, write Fn = Fn−1 ∗ ⟨ t⟩. Extending an automorphism of Fn−1 by fixing 

t defines a homomorphism 

Aut( Fn−1) −→ Out( Fn). (15.24) It is injective. If such an extension were conjugation by w ∈ Fn, then its fixing t would imply 

w ∈ CFn (t) = ⟨t⟩. Write w = tk. If k̸ = 0 and 1̸ = x ∈ Fn−1, the reduced word tkxt −k does not belong to the free factor Fn−1, whereas the extended automorphism sends x into that factor. Hence k = 0 , and the original automorphism is trivial. Thus (15.24) is injective. When 

n ≥ 4, its source is nonlinear by Formanek–Procesi [ 20 ], so Out (Fn) is nonlinear. The outer embedding supplied by Theorem 15.11 then proves nonlinearity of Out( HΓn ). □

These examples do not conflict with Section 6: that section identifies the image of the cohomological representation up to commensurability, not the full automorphism or outer automorphism group. 15.5. Comparison of the examples. The preceding examples show that the finiteness properties of HΓ, IAut (HΓ), Aut (HΓ), and Out (HΓ) need not vary in parallel. The results proved above are summarized in Table 1.

Appendix A. Uniform period isolation in right-angled Artin groups 

This appendix supplies the uniform power-word isolation used in the IA rigidity theorem of Section 7. We first extract a selective, one-period shortening statement from the symbolic rewriting theorem of Lohrey–Stober–Weiß [ 29 ]. We then replace single-letter periods by con-nected composite periods in a faithfully enlarged right-angled Artin group. Finally, successive shortening and a subsequence argument isolate one primitive period class. Throughout, a block means a Servatius block, and no connectedness assumption is made on the defining graph of the ambient right-angled Artin group. The only results used from Lohrey–Stober–Weiß [ 29 ] are Lemma 50(4), Lemmas 53–54, Definition 56, and Lemmas 62–63. They apply to one preprocessed symbolic trace and one selected canonical period. Lemma A.2 records the selective form needed below, and Lemma A.3 verifies that shortening does not delete a powered occurrence. All uniform statements are proved in this appendix. The blow-up Lemma A.5 treats single-letter periods, Lemma A.6 establishes one preprocessing for an entire N -family, and Lemma A.7 justifies successive shortening while the displayed representative remains fixed. These results lead to Proposition A.9; Corollary A.10 and Lemma A.11 are the two consequences used in Theorem 7.5. None of these uniform conclusions is quoted from [29]. A.1. One-period shortening and a fixed representative. We recall the part of the rewriting theory in [ 29 ] that will be used. Let G be a graph product with σ vertex groups, and consider a power word after the preprocessing of that paper. Its power periods belong to the canonical set Ω of cyclic-normal, primitive, connected, composite traces. us.A selective shortening in the class q changes only exponent coordinates of slots labeled by q.Between shortening stages we apply no commutation move, reduction, amalgamation, or new canonical normalization. Thus throughout this appendix the terms “ordered” and “adjacent” refer to this fixed displayed sequence of connecting words and period slots. Lemma A.3 shows that the shortening operation never deletes a slot.

The following is the sign calculation from [ 29 , Lemma 57 and Definition 56]; we state it separately for the successive-shortening argument. 

Lemma A.3. If yi̸ = 0 , then the shortened exponent in (A.4) satisfies 

sgn( zi) = sgn( yi), 1 ≤ | zi| ≤ | yi|.

In particular, selective shortening never creates a zero exponent. Proof. Assume first that yi > 0. The intervals indexed by Ci(u) are pairwise disjoint integer intervals strictly contained between the two integer prefix sums ηi−1 

> p

(u) and ηip(u). Their total number of integer points is therefore at most yi − 1. Formula (A.4) gives 

1 ≤ zi = yi − ∑

> j∈Ci(u)

dj ≤ yi.

The case yi < 0 is the same after reversing the interval: the total removed length is at most 

|yi| − 1, and the sign remains negative. □

Remark A.4 . The conclusion in Lemma A.2 is a triviality-preserving replacement . In general one does not have Sq(u) = Gu. The two-sided statement (A.6) , rather than equality of the represented elements, is what permits successive applications to different period classes. A.2. A faithful blow-up for single-letter periods. Lemma A.2 requires composite periods. The following elementary enlargement applies to primitive blocks whose cyclic core is a single vertex. Let Λ be a finite graph. For each v ∈ V (Λ) , introduce a new vertex tv. Define ̂ Λ on 

V (̂Λ ) = {v, t v : v ∈ V (Λ) } as follows. There is no edge inside the fiber {v, t v}. If vw ∈ EΛ,join every vertex of {v, t v} to every vertex of {w, t w}, and add no other edges. Define 

ι : AΛ −→ Â Λ, ι(v) = vt v, ρ : Â Λ −→ AΛ, ρ(v) = v, ρ(tv) = 1 . (A.10) 

Lemma A.5. The maps in (A.10) are homomorphisms and ρι = id AΛ ; in particular, ι is injective. If p is a cyclically reduced primitive connected Servatius block in AΛ, then ι(p) is a cyclically reduced primitive connected composite Servatius block in Â Λ. Moreover, for primitive connected blocks p, q and each ε ∈ { 1, −1},

ι(p) is conjugate to ι(q)ε ⇐⇒ p is conjugate to qε. (A.11) 

Thus the blow-up reflects both oriented periods and period classes, and every equality proved among image elements descends under ρ.96 J. LEI 

Proof. If vw ∈ EΛ, the two fibers over v and w commute completely, so vt v commutes with 

wt w. Hence ι respects the defining relations of AΛ. The assignments defining ρ also respect all commutation relations, and ρ(vt v) = v. This proves ρι = id and the injectivity of ι.For the normal-form assertions, view Â Λ as the graph product over Λ whose vertex group over v is Fv = F (v, t v). If a reduced coarse syllable word for p has syllables vn1 

> 1

, . . . , v nk 

> k

, its image has syllables (vitvi )ni ∈ Fvi . Each image syllable is a nontrivial reduced word in its free fiber, and the coarse syllable pattern is unchanged. Graph-product normal form [ 43 ] therefore shows that the expanded image word is reduced. Since p is cyclically reduced, the same coarse argument applies to p2; hence ι(p)2 is reduced, and ι(p) is cyclically reduced. The noncommutation graph on supp ι(p) is obtained from that on supp p by replacing every support vertex v with the noncommuting pair v, t v and every old noncommutation edge with all four cross-edges. It is therefore connected. It has at least two vertices even when p is a single vertex, so the image block is composite. If ι(p) = ad with |d| > 1, then p = ρ(a)d, contrary to the primitivity of p. Thus ι(p) is primitive. The implication from right to left in (A.11) follows by applying the homomorphism 

ι to a conjugacy, while the implication from left to right follows by applying the retraction ρ.Applying ρ likewise carries every equality in the image back to AΛ. □

A.3. Uniform preprocessing, iteration, and isolation. We now derive the form used in the IA argument. Recall that a period class is a conjugacy-up-to-inversion class of primitive blocks, whereas an oriented period does not allow inversion. We first isolate, as a separate statement, the fact that the six preprocessing steps of [ 29 ,Section 5.3.1] can be carried out uniformly across the whole family. 

Lemma A.6. Let r1, . . . , r s be fixed primitive Servatius blocks, let c0, . . . , c s be fixed connecting words, let εi ∈ { 1, −1}, and put 

WN = c0rε1N 

> 1

c1 · · · rεsNs cs (N ≥ 1) . (A.12) 

After applying the blow-up of Lemma A.5, the six preprocessing steps can be performed once and for all for the entire family. More precisely, there are fixed canonical periods pi ∈ Ω, fixed signs δi ∈ { 1, −1}, and fixed symbolic connecting words ui such that the preprocessed family has the following fixed symbolic representative 

u(N ) = u0pδ1ε1N 

> 1

u1 · · · pδsεsNs us. (A.13) 

For the chosen displayed representative, the ordered period slots, their canonical period classes, the words ui, the independence relation on the symbolic alphabet, |u(N )|∆, and μ(u(N )) are independent of N . In particular, no preprocessing step introduces an N -dependent multiplier in an unbounded exponent or an N -dependent cancellation of a period slot. Proof. For each i, choose once and for all a cyclically reduced primitive connected core ai

and a conjugator gi with ri = giaig−1 

> i

. Lemma A.5 makes ι(ai) cyclically reduced, primitive, connected, and composite, and it preserves conjugacy up to either orientation. We verify that the six preprocessing steps of [29, Section 5.3.1] are uniform in N .Step 1: Cyclic reduction. The period word attached to the ith slot is fixed before the exponent is read. Hence its cyclic core and the conjugator used to expose that core are fixed. Replacing a power by a conjugate of the power inserts only fixed conjugating words, which are absorbed into the adjacent connecting words. The factor N remains solely in the exponent. Step 2: Connected decomposition. The image core ι(ai) is connected by Lemma A.5, so this step produces one connected factor rather than an N -dependent list. More AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 97 

generally, even without the blow-up the connected components depend only on the fixed period word and not on its exponent. Step 3: Single-letter powers. Every image period is composite by Lemma A.5. Thus no unbounded powered slot is moved into the simple-power part of a connecting word. The blow-up is used here to ensure that every unbounded period remains a period letter rather than becoming part of a connecting word. Step 4: Normal forms for letters. Only finitely many letters occur in the fixed image periods. Choose the representatives prescribed in [ 29 , Step 4 of Section 5.3.1] once for that finite list. The replacement is letterwise and therefore independent of N .Step 5: Primitive cyclic normal forms. The cyclic-normal representative and its conjugator depend only on the fixed period word. The primitive-root substep of [ 29 , Step 5 of Section 5.3.1] would replace qx by rkx when q = rk. Here k = 1 : the image core is primitive, and conjugacy preserves primitivity in a right-angled Artin group. Hence the exponent remains exactly εiN , up to the orientation chosen in the next step. Step 6: Passage to Ω. Choose the canonical representative pi ∈ Ω of the period class. The conjugator and the orientation sign δi depend only on the fixed period. The possible ambiguity mentioned in [ 29 , Remark 44] is absent for right-angled Artin groups, whose vertex groups contain no nontrivial element of order two; equivalently, the orientation may be fixed consistently once for each occurrence. Since εi is fixed in (A.12), the conjugator selected for that orientation is also independent of N .After these steps, absorb all fixed conjugators and the bounded phase words from the symbolic encoding into the neighboring connecting words. Reduce those connecting words once; they contain no occurrence of N . At this stage every powered exponent is ±N and hence is nonzero. We retain every powered occurrence as a separate slot in the chosen symbolic trace; no rewriting or rule-(1) amalgamation is performed between the preprocessing and the shortening steps. Definition 56 and Lemmas 62–63 of [ 29 ] apply to this symbolic trace and do not require it to be irreducible. We now fix this displayed representative and use it at every later stage; no further commutation moves or normalization are allowed. This gives (A.13) . The symbolic length counts slots rather than exponent size, and μ is the maximum length of the fixed canonical periods. Both are independent of N , as are the symbolic alphabet and its independence relation. Lemma A.3 will ensure that all of these slots remain nonzero throughout the successive shortening process. □

Lemma A.7. Let 

u(0) = u0px1 

> 1

u1 · · · pxs 

> s

us

be a realization of a fixed preprocessed representative, with every xi̸ = 0 . Let q1, . . . , q t ∈ Ω be pairwise distinct canonical periods. For each j, compute the carving intervals for qj from the current word u(j−1) and put 

u(j) = Sqj (u(j−1) ), 1 ≤ j ≤ t. 

Then, for every j:

(i) u(j) has exactly the same ordered period slots, period labels, connecting words, symbolic alphabet, and independence relation as u(0) ;

(ii) every exponent remains nonzero; exponents in the selected class retain their signs, and exponents outside that class are unchanged; 

(iii) |u(j)|∆ = |u(0) |∆ and μ(u(j)) = μ(u(0) ), so the bound in (A.9) is uniform at every stage; 

(iv) 

π(u(j−1) ) = G1 ⇐⇒ π(u(j)) = G1.98 J. LEI 

A period class already shortened is never altered at a later stage. Therefore, the periods may be shortened successively without repeating any preprocessing or introducing a normalization step between stages. Proof. Proceed by induction on j. Definition 56 of [29] changes only exponent coordinates of letters in ∆qj ∪ ∆−1 

> qj

and leaves every connecting word and every other period letter unchanged in the chosen displayed representative. By Lemma A.3, each changed exponent is still nonzero and retains its sign. Thus no slot is deleted and the exact displayed representative persists, proving (i) and (ii). No equality is obtained by commuting symbolic letters: every stage uses the same displayed word representative fixed before the first shortening. Since no powered slot is deleted and the symbolic subalphabets ∆q ∪ ∆−1 

> q

attached to distinct canonical periods are disjoint by construction, the current word is again a symbolic trace of the form required in Definition 56 for every period class not yet processed. Thus the next application of Lemma 62 is legitimate without a new preprocessing or normalization step. Since the symbolic letters and period words are unchanged, the parameters |u|∆ and μ(u) are unchanged as well, proving (iii). Lemma 62 of [ 29 ], namely (A.6) , gives (iv) for the carving family computed from the current word. Finally, the qj are pairwise distinct, so a later shortening never selects a previously treated period class. The induction proves all assertions. □

Example A.8 (Three period classes) . Consider the family 

u(N ) = a0pN a1q−N a2p−N a3rN a4qN a5,

with fixed connecting words and three distinct canonical periods p, q, r . To isolate p, first shorten the two q-slots. Their exponents become bounded nonzero integers b1(N ), b 2(N ), while both p-slots, the r-slot, and every ai remain unchanged in the chosen displayed representative. Next shorten the r-slot, obtaining a bounded nonzero integer c(N ); the already shortened 

q-coordinates are not revisited. The resulting family has the same skeleton: 

a0pN a1qb1(N )a2p−N a3rc(N )a4qb2(N )a5.

Passing to an unbounded subsequence makes b1, b 2, c constant, while the two p-exponents remain exactly N and −N . This is the finite-class argument used in Proposition A.9. 

Proposition A.9. Let G be a right-angled Artin group, let r1, . . . , r s be primitive Servatius blocks, and let 

WN = c0rε1N 

> 1

c1 · · · rεsNs cs, εi ∈ { 1, −1}, (A.14) 

where the connecting words ci are fixed. Suppose that WN = 1 for an unbounded set N of positive integers. Fix one period class P occurring in (A.14) . After passing to an unbounded subset N ′ ⊆ N , the normalized family can be replaced, through transformations that preserve triviality in both directions and do not change its ordered power-word skeleton, by a family with the following properties: 

(i) every occurrence belonging to P is untouched, so relative to its original oriented base 

ri its exponent is still εiN ; equivalently, after canonical normalization its exponent is 

δiεiN for a fixed δi ∈ { 1, −1};

(ii) every exponent belonging to every other period class is constant, independently of 

N ∈ N ′.The replacement is triviality-preserving; it is not asserted to represent the same element as 

WN when that element is nontrivial. Proof. By Lemma A.6, after the blow-up of Lemma A.5 and canonical normalization the family ι(WN ) has a fixed symbolic skeleton whose connecting words and canonical periods are independent of N , and whose exponents are still exactly ±N .AUTOMORPHISMS OF BESTVINA–BRADY GROUPS 99 

List the finitely many canonical period classes other than P and shorten them successively. At each stage compute the carving intervals for the selected period from the current word. Lemma A.7 shows that the exact ordered skeleton, every P -exponent, and every previously treated exponent remain unchanged. The shortening parameters are the fixed parameters of the original skeleton, so (A.9) gives one uniform bound, independent of N , for every exponent outside P . Triviality is preserved in both directions at every stage. After all classes other than P have been treated, every exponent outside P ranges over a finite set. There are only finitely many such occurrences, so successive applications of the infinite pigeonhole principle give an unbounded subset N ′ on which all of them are constant. The target exponents remain the original δiεiN . The retraction ρ is used only after an equality has been obtained in the enlarged RAAG; by Lemma A.5, all such equalities descend to G. □

Corollary A.10. Under the hypotheses of Proposition A.9, no primitive period class occurs exactly once in (A.14) .Proof. If a class occurred once, isolate it. In the enlarged RAAG the resulting identities would have the form Ap εN B = 1 for a primitive period p, a sign ε ∈ { 1, −1}, fixed elements A, B ,and all N in an unbounded set. Comparing two distinct surviving values N and M gives 

pε(N −M ) = 1 , contrary to torsion-freeness of a right-angled Artin group. □

Lemma A.11. Assume the hypotheses of Proposition A.9. Suppose that one period class occurs exactly twice, in adjacent factors b−N aN , and that the primitive blocks a and b have the same oriented primitive period. Then a = b.Proof. Write G = AΛ and work after the blow-up. Choose the canonical representative p of the common period class. The oriented-period hypothesis gives a common sign δ ∈ { 1, −1}

and elements g, h such that 

ι(b) = gp δg−1, ι(a) = hp δh−1.

The adjacent product itself has the explicit form 

ι(b)−N ι(a)N = (gp δg−1)−N (hp δh−1)N

= gp −δN g−1hp δN h−1

= gp −δN (g−1h)pδN h−1.

Thus normalization absorbs the outer factors g and h−1 into the neighboring fixed connecting words, while the unique connecting word between the two displayed p-powers is C = g−1h.Isolating this class keeps every other period exponent constant, and selectivity leaves C

unchanged in the chosen displayed representative. Thus, on an unbounded set of exponents, one obtains 

Ap −δN Cp δN B = 1 , (A.15) where A, B, C are independent of N .Compare (A.15) for two distinct surviving values N and M . Cancelling the fixed outer factors gives 

pδ(M −N )Cp δ(N −M ) = C. 

Hence C centralizes the nonzero power pδ(M −N ). The Servatius centralizer theorem [ 43 ] gives 

CÂΛ

(pd) = CÂΛ

(p) for every d̸ = 0 , so C commutes with p. Since h = gC , it follows that 

hp δh−1 = gp δg−1.

Thus ι(a) = ι(b), and applying the retraction ρ gives a = b. □100 J. LEI
